Thus we obtained the map we were looking for under the hypothesis that
K
is a number field. The usefulness of this strategy lies in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.2.1
.

If
K
is a number field, and
X
is smooth and proper, then the diagram in figure
4
is commutative.

Proof.

This is an instance of the more general compatibility of the edge maps with cup products and push-forwards.
∎

When
X
is smooth and affine (but not necessarily proper), its base change to an algebraically closed field verifies

H^{i}_{\et}(X_{\overline{K}},\mathbb{Q}_{p})=0\quad\forall i>2d.

Therefore the first part of the above argument still applies and proves the following.

Proposition 3.2.2
.

If
X
is smooth and affine over a number field, then there is a morphism

H_{\et}^{2d+1}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2d-j))\to H^{1}(K,H_{\et}^{2d}(X_{\overline{K}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2d-j))).

Suppose now that
K
is a
p
-adic field, i.e. a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. In this case we cannot invoke the notion of “pure”
G_{K}
-representation, so we cannot argue for the vanishing of
E_{2}^{0,2d+1}
. However, when
K=K_{v}
is the localisation of a number field at a place
v
over
p
, we have localisation maps induced by
K\to K_{v}
which are compatible with the edge maps:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2d+1}_{\et}(X_{K},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2d-j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(K,H^{2d+1}_{\et}(X_{\overline{K}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2d-j)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2d+1}_{\et}(X_{K_{v}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2d-j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(K_{v},H^{2d+1}_{\et}(X_{\overline{K_{v}}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2d-j)))}

By the previous argument, if
X
is smooth and proper over
K
then the top right group is trivial. Therefore every element in the cohomology of
X_{K_{v}}
that is the localisation of a global class is still in the kernel of the bottom edge map. As the kernel coincides with
F^{1}H_{v}
for
H_{v}=H^{2d+1}_{\et}(X_{K_{v}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2d-j))
, it is equipped with the sought morphism to
E_{2}^{1,2d}
. This is the strategy we will use in Subsection
7.2
.

Figure 1:
Compatibility of
r_{\mathcal{D}}
with cup product and push-forward

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2d+1}_{\mot}(X,2d-j)\times H^{2d}_{\mot}(X,d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{4d+1}_{\mot}(X,3d-j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec K,d-j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathcal{D}}^{2d+1}(X_{\mathbb{C}},K(2d-j))\times H_{\mathcal{D}}^{2d}(X_{\mathbb{C}},K(d))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathcal{D}}^{4d+1}(X_{\mathbb{C}},K(3d-j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Spec\mathbb{C},K(d-j))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{D}}\times r_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}

Figure 2:
Complex diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2d+1}_{\mot}(X,2d-j)\times H^{2d}_{\mot}(X,d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{4d+1}_{\mot}(X,3d-j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec K,d-j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathcal{D}}^{2d+1}(X_{\mathbb{C}},K(2d-j))\times H_{\mathcal{D}}^{2d}(X_{\mathbb{C}},K(d))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathcal{D}}^{4d+1}(X_{\mathbb{C}},K(3d-j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(\Spec\mathbb{C},K(d-j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K}}(K,H_{B}^{2d}(X,K(2d-j)))\times\Ext^{0}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K}}(K,H_{B}^{2d}(X,K(d)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K}}(K,H_{B}^{4d}(X,K(3d-j)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(\Spec\mathbb{C},K(d-j))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{D}}\times r_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}

Figure 3:
Compatibility of
r_{\et}
with cup product and push-forward

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2d+1}_{\mot}(X,2d-j)\times H^{2d}_{\mot}(X,d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{4d+1}_{\mot}(X,3d-j)}

p
-adic étale diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2d+1}_{\mot}(X,2d-j)\times H^{2d}_{\mot}(X,d)}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}

4
Cohomology of Kuga-Sato varieties

In this section we introduce the key geometric objects of this paper, i.e. Kuga-Sato varieties, along with their cohomology. They were introduced and studied in
[
Del71
,
Sch90
]
—which are also the main references—in the general case, and in the appendix of
[
BDP13
]
by Brian Conrad for the case of level
\Gamma_{1}(N)
. From now on we assume
N\geq 5
so that all the objects we construct exist.

4.1
Motivic cohomology

Let
Y_{1}(N)
be the moduli space of elliptic curves with a point of order
N
. The curve
Y_{1}(N)
has a model over
\mathbb{Q}
which becomes isomorphic to a finite number of copies of
\Gamma_{1}(N)\backslash\mathcal{H}
when base changed to
\mathbb{C}
.

Let
\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\pi}Y_{1}(N)
be the universal elliptic curve. For every
d\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
we can form the
d
-th fibre product of
\mathcal{E}
over the modular curve, where in every component the map along which we are taking the fibre product is the projection
\pi
. We have thus a variety:

\mathcal{E}^{d}\xrightarrow{\pi_{d}}Y_{1}(N).

\mathcal{E}^{d}
is
d
-dimensional over
Y_{1}(N)
and
(d+1)
-dimensional over
\Spec\mathbb{Q}
. We can further form the fibre product
\mathcal{E}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}^{d}
, where this time we consider both copies of
\mathcal{E}^{d}
as varieties over
\Spec\mathbb{Q}
. We have then a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}(N)\times Y_{1}(N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{d}\times\pi_{d}}

Let
X_{1}(N)
be the Baily-Borel compactification of
Y_{1}(N)
. Notice that with our choice of model for
Y_{1}(N)
, the cusp at infinity of
X_{1}(N)
is only defined over
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
, even though
X_{1}(N)
is still defined over
\mathbb{Q}
.

One can consider the universal generalised elliptic curve
\overline{\mathcal{E}}\to X_{1}(N)
and repeat the process to have proper varieties. However, the fibres of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
over the cusps are singular cubics, so
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{d}
fails to be smooth when
d>1
. In
[
Del71
]
Deligne proved the existence of a
canonical
desingularisation which we denote with
W_{d}
:

W_{d}\to\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{d}\to X_{1}(N).

This comes equipped with a structural morphism
\overline{\pi}\colon W_{d}\to X_{1}(N)
, and is a smooth and proper variety over
X_{1}(N)
. Define also the
cuspidal locus

W_{d}^{\infty}=\overline{\pi}^{-1}\bigl(X_{1}(N)\setminus Y_{1}(N)\bigr)=W_{d}\times\{\mathrm{cusps}\}
. Notice that
\overline{\pi}^{-1}(Y_{1}(N))=\mathcal{E}^{d}
.

We also denote with
\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{d}
the locus of
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{d}
where the projection to
X_{1}(N)
is smooth, i.e. the Néron model of
\mathcal{E}^{d}
over
X_{1}(N)
, and with
\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{d,*}
the connected component of the identity.

Definition 4.1.1
.

The
Kuga-Sato variety

\mathscr{W}_{d}
is the self-fibre product of
W_{d}
over the base scheme
\Spec\mathbb{Q}

\mathscr{W}_{d}\to X_{1}(N)^{2},\quad\mathscr{W}_{d}=W_{d}\times_{\Spec\mathbb{Q}}W_{d}.

Define also the
cuspidal locus

\mathscr{W}_{d}^{\infty}=(\overline{\pi}\times\overline{\pi})^{-1}\bigl(X_{1}(N)^{2}\setminus Y_{1}(N)^{2}\bigr)
.

All the varieties just introduced are defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. Kuga-Sato varieties are both smooth and proper over the base scheme
\Spec\mathbb{Q}
, as are the
W_{d}
. Hence their cohomology groups enjoy several desirable properties. This is the one of the two main motivations for the introduction of Kuga-Sato varieties. The second is that they trivialise the coefficient sheaves on
X_{1}(N)^{2}
whose cocycles are given by cusp forms, as we explain below. Kuga-Sato varieties are the closest we can get to
\mathcal{E}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}^{d}
while keeping both the smooth and proper requirements. At this stage one would like to link the cohomology of
\mathscr{W}_{d}
to that of
\mathcal{E}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}^{d}
, in order to pass easily from classes which are easier to construct to classes which enjoy more cohomological properties. The best result in this direction was proved by Brunault and Chida.

Let
S_{d}
be the group of permutations on
d
objects. There is an obvious action of
S_{d}
on the fibre product
\mathcal{E}^{d}
given by permuting the coordinates. Likewise, the group
\mu_{2}
acts on
\mathcal{E}
as the multiplication by
-1
, and this of course extends to an action of
\mu_{2}^{d}
on
\mathcal{E}^{d}
. We gather these together into an action of the group
\mu_{2}^{d}\rtimes S_{d}=\mathfrak{I}_{d}
.

Definition 4.1.2
.

The character
\varepsilon_{d}
of
\mathfrak{I}_{d}
is defined by:

\varepsilon_{d}\colon\mathfrak{I}_{d}\to\mu_{2},\quad(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d},\sigma)\mapsto a_{1}\cdots a_{d}\cdot\sgn(\sigma).

Theorem 4.1.3

(
[
BC16
, Proposition 8.1]
)
.

For every quadruple
(i,d,d^{\prime},j)\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
with
d,d^{\prime}\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle H_{\mot}^{i}(W_{d}\times W_{d^{\prime}},\mathbb{Q}(j))(\varepsilon_{d},\varepsilon_{d^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\simeq H_{\mot}^{i}(\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{d,*}\times\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{d^{\prime},*},\mathbb{Q}(j))(\varepsilon_{d},\varepsilon_{d^{\prime}})

in particular

\displaystyle H_{\mot}^{i}(\mathscr{W}_{d},\mathbb{Q}(j))(\varepsilon_{d},\varepsilon_{d})

\displaystyle\simeq H_{\mot}^{i}(\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{d,*}\times\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{d,*},\mathbb{Q}(j))(\varepsilon_{d},\varepsilon_{d}).

Remark
.

Since the desingularisation is canonical, the Hecke correspondences on
X_{1}(N)
associated to the Hecke operators extend as correspondences on
W_{d}
, as explained in
[
Sch90
, §4]
. In particular they act on the cohomology of
W_{d}
.

4.1.1
The coefficient sheaf

Let
\mathcal{T}\in\{\et,\dR,\mathrm{Betti},\mathcal{D},\syn,\rig\}
be a cohomology theory. Recall that
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}
denotes the standard notion of “constant” sheaf in
\mathcal{T}
. Let
\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})
be the sheaf on
Y_{1}(N)
defined as
(R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}})(1)
. This can be described explicitly as “cohomology along the fibres” by the assignment

U\mapsto H_{\mathcal{T}}^{1}(\pi^{-1}(U),\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}|_{\pi^{-1}(U)})(1).

In particular the fibre at a point
x\in Y_{1}(N)
is
H_{\mathcal{T}}^{1}(\mathcal{E}_{x},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}})(1)
. For every field
K/\mathbb{Q}
, we denote
\mathscr{H}_{K}(\mathcal{E})=\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})\otimes K
the extension to
K
. This sheaf constitutes the “coefficients” of the cohomology groups where the
\mathcal{T}
-realisations of Eisenstein motivic classes live, if we wish to work over modular curves. Indeed, a result of Scholl states that–roughly speaking–the cohomology of
\mathcal{E}^{d}
with trivial coefficients is equivalent to the cohomology of the modular curve
Y_{1}(N)
with coefficients in a suitably defined sheaf
\TSym^{d}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})
.

We now explain concretely Scholl’s result by means of Liebermann’s trick, a manipulation coming from the analysis of the action of permutations on Kuga-Sato varieties. As before
\varepsilon_{d}
is a character of
\mathfrak{I}_{d}
, which acts on
\mathcal{E}^{d}
and
W_{d}
. If
H
is an abelian group, the group
S_{d}
acts also on the direct product
H^{d}
in a natural way. Define
\TSym^{d}H\subseteq H^{d}
as the subgroup of invariants under this action, and
\Sym^{d}H
as the coinvariants.

Proposition 4.1.4

(
[
Sch90
, Proposition 4.1.1]
)
.

For every cohomology theory
\mathcal{T}\in\{\et,\dR,\allowbreak\mathrm{Betti},\allowbreak\mathcal{D},\syn,\rig\}
, there is an isomorphism:

H_{\mathcal{T}}^{i}(Y_{1}(N),\TSym^{d}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(j))\simeq H_{\mathcal{T}}^{i+d}(\mathcal{E}^{d},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}(j+d))(\varepsilon_{d}).

Motivic cohomology should be the initial object in the category of “cohomology theories”; this motivates the following definition.

Definition 4.1.5
.

Motivic cohomology of modular curves with coefficients is defined by
H_{\mot}^{i}(Y_{1}(N),\TSym^{d}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(j))=H_{\mot}^{i+d}(\mathcal{E}^{d},\mathbb{Q}(j+d))(\varepsilon_{d}).

This description is the same outlined in
[
KLZ15
]
. In particular, the regulator maps
r_{\mathcal{T}}
commute with the action of the character
\varepsilon_{d}
, so they go through to

r_{\mathcal{T}}\colon H^{i}_{\mot}(Y_{1}(N),\TSym^{d}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(j))\to H^{i}_{\mathcal{T}}(Y_{1}(N),\TSym^{d}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(j)).

By
[
Anc15
, Théorème 1.3]
, the sheaf
\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})
exists as a motivic sheaf over
Y_{1}(N)
, and so do its symmetric tensors. Hence we can make sense of the usual homological constructions. Denote
\TSym^{[d,d^{\prime}]}=\TSym^{d}\otimes\TSym^{d^{\prime}}
, then together with the above definition we have the following proposition.

Proposition 4.1.6
.

The following equality holds:

H^{i}_{\mot}(Y_{1}(N)^{2},\TSym^{[d,d^{\prime}]}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(j))=H^{i+d+d^{\prime}}_{\mot}(\mathcal{E}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}^{d},\mathbb{Q}(j+d+d^{\prime}))(\varepsilon_{d},\varepsilon_{d^{\prime}}).

Proof.

Thanks to
[
Anc15
, Théorème 1.3]
the sheaf on left hand side exists as a motivic sheaf over
Y_{1}(N)
, and the motivic cohomology group is in the category of (relative) Chow motives. By
[
Anc15
, §2.2]
, in this category the Künneth formula holds, so we can compute:

\displaystyle\begin{lgathered}H^{i}_{\mot}(Y_{1}(N)^{2},\TSym^{[d,d^{\prime}]}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(j))\\
=\bigoplus_{p=0}^{i}H^{p}_{\mot}(Y_{1}(N),\TSym^{d}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(j))\otimes H^{i-p}_{\mot}(Y_{1}(N),\TSym^{d^{\prime}}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E}))\\
=\bigoplus_{p=0}^{i}H^{p+d}_{\mot}(\mathcal{E}^{d},\mathbb{Q}(j+d))(\varepsilon_{d})\otimes H^{i+d^{\prime}-p}_{\mot}(\mathcal{E}^{d^{\prime}},\mathbb{Q}(d^{\prime}))(\varepsilon_{d^{\prime}})\\
=H^{i+d+d^{\prime}}_{\mot}(\mathcal{E}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}^{d},\mathbb{Q}(j+d+d^{\prime}))(\varepsilon_{d},\varepsilon_{d^{\prime}})\end{lgathered}

which proves the claim.
∎

Carrying forward these ideas, we give the next definition.

Definition 4.1.7
.

Define motivic cohomology groups as follows:

H^{i}_{\mot}(X_{1}(N)^{2},\TSym^{[d,d^{\prime}]}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(j))=H^{i+d+d^{\prime}}_{\mot}(W_{d}\times W_{d^{\prime}},\mathbb{Q}(j+d+d^{\prime}))(\varepsilon_{d},\varepsilon_{d^{\prime}}).

4.2
Motives for cusp forms and Rankin-Selberg convolution

In this subsection we identify “pieces” of various cohomology groups that are naturally associated to cusp forms and their convolutions. This is important for our work, since these objects are smaller than the whole cohomology groups but still retain all the information we need. We follow
[
Sch90
, §4]
.

Recall that by the remark at page
Remark
, the Hecke operators act on the cohomology of
W_{k}
and
W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}}
. For any Hecke operator
T
, denote with
T^{\prime}
the dual Hecke operator, i.e. its adjoint with respect to the Petersson inner product. When
l
is a prime not dividing the level,
T_{l}^{\prime}=T_{l}\langle l\rangle^{-1}
.

From now on, we denote with
\etbar
étale cohomology over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Definition 4.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{T}\in\{\dR,B,\etbar,\rig\}
. Define:

•

M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)
as the maximal
K_{f}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}
-submodule of

H^{k+1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{E}^{k},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}})(\varepsilon_{k})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}K_{f}

on which the Hecke operators
T_{l}
act as multiplication by
a_{l}(f)
for all primes
l
;

•

M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)^{*}
as the maximal quotient of

H^{k+1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{E}^{k},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}(k+1))(\varepsilon_{k})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}K_{f}

on which the dual Hecke operators
T_{l}^{\prime}
act as multiplication by
a_{l}(f)
for all primes
l
;

Both
M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)
and
M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)^{*}
are
2
-dimensional.

The different realisations enjoy extra structure:

•

M_{\dR}(f)
and
M_{\dR}(f)^{*}
are filtered
K_{f}
-vector spaces;

•

M_{B}(f)
and
M_{B}(f)^{*}
are pure Hodge structures over
K_{f}
of weight
k+1
and
-(k+1)
respectively, and whose only non-zero Hodge numbers are
h^{k+1,0}
,
h^{0,k+1}
, and
h^{-(k+1),0}
,
h^{0,-(k+1)}
respectively;

•

M_{\etbar}(f)
and
M_{\etbar}(f)^{*}
are pure
p
-adic Galois representations of weight
k+1
and
-(k+1)
respectively, over
K_{f}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\simeq(K_{f})_{\mathfrak{p}}
for some prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}\mid p
. They are
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-representations, as the varieties are defined over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Remark
.

The extra twist in the definition of
M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)^{*}
is explained by the aim to define
M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)^{*}
such that it is naturally the dual of
M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)
. There is a natural pairing

H^{k+1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{E}^{k},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}})\times H^{k+1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{E}^{k},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}})\to H^{0}_{\mathcal{T}}(\Spec\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}(-k-1))=0

so in order to land in the more natural
H^{0}_{\mathcal{T}}(\Spec\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}})\simeq\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}
, we need an extra twist to compensate for the one on the target.

An important property that these modules enjoy is that they can be found in several cohomology groups. Indeed, one can give the same definition for the compactified variety
W_{k}
, and it turns out that the resulting objects are canonically isomorphic to those we defined. Additionally, over
\mathcal{E}^{k}
there is a canonical lift of
M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)
to compactly supported cohomology (this statement makes sense only over a non-compact variety). To sum up, there are
canonical
isomorphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c,\mathcal{T}}^{k+1}(\mathcal{E}^{k},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}})(\varepsilon_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathcal{T}}^{k+1}(\mathcal{E}^{k},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}})(\varepsilon_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathcal{T}}^{k+1}(W_{k},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}})(\varepsilon_{k})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

And:

H_{\mathcal{T}}^{k+1}(\mathcal{E}^{k},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}(k+1))(\varepsilon_{k})\twoheadrightarrow M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)^{*}\simeq M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)^{*}\twoheadleftarrow H_{\mathcal{T}}^{k+1}(W_{k},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}(k+1))(\varepsilon_{k}).

Since these are all canonical, we will use the identifications in the rest of the paper. In particular, any cohomology class in
M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)
has an associated canonical cohomology class with compact support over
\mathcal{E}^{k}
.

We also have comparison isomorphisms linking the various realisations. In particular, we have the de Rham, crystalline and Faltings-Tsuji comparison theorems:

(De Rham)

\displaystyle M_{B}(f)\otimes\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\simeq M_{\dR}(f)\otimes\mathbb{C},

(
C_{\mathrm{crys}}
)

\displaystyle M_{\rig}(f)\otimes B_{\mathrm{crys}}

\displaystyle\simeq M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\otimes B_{\mathrm{crys}}\simeq M_{\etbar}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\otimes B_{\mathrm{crys}},

(
C_{\dR}
)

\displaystyle M_{\rig}(f)\otimes B_{\dR}

\displaystyle\simeq M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\otimes B_{\dR}\simeq M_{\etbar}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\otimes B_{\dR}.

For the Rankin convolution one defines the motives using the above ones as building blocks.

Definition 4.2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{T}\in\{\dR,B,\etbar,\rig\}
. Define:

•

M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes g)=M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)\otimes M_{\mathcal{T}}(g)
;

•

M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes g)^{*}=M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)^{*}\otimes M_{\mathcal{T}}(g)^{*}
.

Both
M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes g)
and
M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes g)^{*}
are
4
-dimensional, as the building blocks have dimension
2
.

By the Künneth formula this definition gives the following characterisations:

•

M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes g)
is the maximal
K_{f,g}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}
-submodule of

H^{k+k^{\prime}+2}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}})(\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k^{\prime}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}K_{f,g}

on which the Hecke operators
(T_{l},1)
and
(1,T_{l})
act as multiplication by
a_{l}(f)
and
a_{l}(g)
respectively, for all primes
l
;

•

M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes g)^{*}
is the maximal quotient of

H^{k+k^{\prime}+2}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}(2+k+k^{\prime}))(\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k^{\prime}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}K_{f,g}

on which the dual Hecke operators
(T_{l}^{\prime},1)
and
(1,T_{l}^{\prime})
act as multiplication by
a_{l}(f)
and
a_{l}(g)
respectively, for all primes
l
;

As before, the different realisations enjoy extra structure:

•

M_{\dR}(f\otimes g)
and
M_{\dR}(f\otimes g)^{*}
are filtered
K_{f,g}
-vector spaces;

•

M_{B}(f\otimes g)
and
M_{B}(f\otimes g)^{*}
are pure Hodge structures over
K_{f,g}
of weight
k+k^{\prime}+2
and
-(k+k^{\prime}+2)
respectively, and whose only non-zero Hodge numbers are

\displaystyle h^{k+k^{\prime}+2,0},\;h^{k+1,k^{\prime}+1}

\displaystyle,\;h^{k^{\prime}+1,k+1},\;h^{0,k+k^{\prime}+2}

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad h^{-(k+k^{\prime}+2),0},\;h^{-(k+1),-(k^{\prime}+1)}

\displaystyle,\;h^{-(k^{\prime}+1),-(k+1)},\;h^{0,-(k+k^{\prime}+2)}

respectively;

•

M_{\etbar}(f\otimes g)
and
M_{\etbar}(f\otimes g)^{*}
are pure
p
-adic Galois representations of weight
k+k^{\prime}+2
and
-(k+k^{\prime}+2)
respectively, over
K_{f,g}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\simeq(K_{f,g})_{\mathcal{P}}
for some prime ideal
\mathcal{P}\mid p
. They are
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-representations, as the varieties are defined over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Similar remarks apply to these spaces: both have a canonical lift to the cohomology of
W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}}
, and
M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes g)
has also a canonical lift to the compactly supported cohomology of
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
. Moreover, they are linked by the de Rham, crystalline and Faltings-Tsuji comparison theorems.

By construction there are natural pairings

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)\times M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)^{*}\to K_{f}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}},

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes g)\times M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes g)^{*}\to K_{f,g}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}.

When
f=g
,
M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes f)
enjoys the standard decomposition in symmetric and antisymmetric tensors:

M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes f)\simeq\Sym^{2}M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)\oplus\wedge^{2}M_{\mathcal{T}}(f).

Let
s\colon M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes f)\to M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes f)
be the involution swapping the components of the tensor product, then the above are by definition the
s=1
and
s=-1
eigenspaces. It is a standard fact that
\Sym^{2}M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)
is
3
-dimensional, while
\wedge^{2}M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)
is
1
-dimensional. This decomposition mirrors (
5
) in various cohomology theories, in particular it is exactly that decomposition of Galois representations when
\mathcal{T}=\etbar
. Correspondingly we have:

(12)

M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes f)^{*}\simeq\Sym^{2}M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)^{*}\oplus\wedge^{2}M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)^{*}.

Notice that by functoriality the projections

\displaystyle\pr_{f}\colon H^{k+1}_{\mathcal{T}}(W_{k},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}(k+1))\twoheadrightarrow M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)^{*},

\displaystyle\pr_{f,g}\colon H^{k+k^{\prime}+2}_{\mathcal{T}}(W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}(2+k+k^{\prime}))\twoheadrightarrow M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes g)^{*}.

induce morphisms in cohomology. Clearly
\pr_{f}
and
\pr_{f,g}
can be defined equivalently from the cohomology of
\mathcal{E}^{k}
and
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
. We particularly highlight the following.

•

in the Betti realisation there are morphisms in the category of mixed Hodge structures:

\displaystyle\Ext_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}^{i}(K_{f},H^{k+1}_{B}(W_{k},\mathbb{Q}(k+1)))\xrightarrow{\pr_{f}}\Ext^{i}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}(K_{f},M_{B}(f)^{*}),

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\Ext_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f,g}}}^{i}(K_{f,g},H^{k+k^{\prime}+2}_{B}(W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}},\mathbb{Q}(2+k+k^{\prime})))\\
\xrightarrow{\pr_{f,g}}\Ext^{i}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f,g}}}(K_{f,g},M_{B}(f\otimes g)^{*}).\end{multlined}

•

in the étale realisation over an algebraic closure, there are morphisms in the category of Galois modules:

\displaystyle H^{i}(\mathbb{Q},H^{k+1}_{\et}(W_{k,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(k+1)))\xrightarrow{\pr_{f}}H^{i}(\mathbb{Q},M_{\etbar}(f)^{*}),

\displaystyle H^{i}(\mathbb{Q},H^{k+k^{\prime}+2}_{\et}((W_{k}\!\times\!W_{k^{\prime}})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2+k+k^{\prime})))\xrightarrow{\pr_{f,g}}H^{i}(\mathbb{Q},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes g)^{*})

When
f=g
, we can compose these with the projections induced by the direct sum decomposition (
12
), to obtain the following morphisms in the category of mixed Hodge structures:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}^{i}(K_{f},H^{2k+2}_{B}(\mathscr{W}_{k},\mathbb{Q}(2k+2)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{i}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}(K_{f},\wedge^{2}M_{B}(f)^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{i}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}(K_{f},M_{B}(f\otimes f)^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{i}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}(K_{f},\Sym^{2}M_{B}(f)^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{f,f}}

and the analogous ones in the category of Galois modules:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(\mathbb{Q},H^{2k+2}_{\et}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2k+2)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(\mathbb{Q},\wedge^{2}M_{\etbar}(f)^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(\mathbb{Q},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(\mathbb{Q},\Sym^{2}M_{\etbar}(f)^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{f,f}}

4.3
De Rham cohomology and modular forms

In this subsection we derive explicit descriptions for the de Rham cohomology of Kuga-Sato varieties. To start with, we have the following description of the canonical filtration restricted to the
\varepsilon_{d}
-eigenspaces, as explained in
[
Kat04
, 202–205]
:

\Fil^{i}H^{d+1}_{\dR}(W_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d})=\begin{cases}H^{d+1}_{\dR}(W_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d})&\text{$i\leq 0$}\\
S_{d+2}(N)&\text{$0<i\leq d+1$}\\
0&\text{$i>d+1$}\end{cases}

which implies that we have cusp forms spaces in degree
d+1
while all the rest is concentrated in degree
0
. Considering the Hodge decomposition:

S_{d+2}(N)=\Fil^{d+1}H^{d+1}_{\dR}(W_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d})\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}p+q=d+1\\
p\geq d+1\end{subarray}}H^{p,q}_{\dR}(W_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d})=H^{d+1,0}_{\dR}(W_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d}).

This also shows
H^{0,d+1}_{\dR}(W_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d})=\overline{S_{d+2}(N)}
. Since there are no graded pieces in degrees
1
to
d
, we deduce

(13)

H^{d+1}_{\dR}(W_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d})\simeq S_{d+2}(N)\oplus\overline{S_{d+2}(N)}.

This direct sum decomposition shows that the
\varepsilon_{d}
-eigenspace of the de Rham cohomology of
W_{d}
only contains classes coming from cusp forms. Since we are working with coefficients in a field, the Künneth formula for
\mathscr{W}_{d}
becomes an isomorphism

H^{q}_{B}(\mathscr{W}_{d},\mathbb{C})\simeq\bigoplus_{i+j=q}H^{i}_{B}(W_{d},\mathbb{C})\otimes H^{j}_{B}(W_{d},\mathbb{C}).

In particular for
i>d+1
we have
H^{i}_{B}(W_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d})=0
, so we get for
q=2d+2
:

H^{2d+2}_{B}(\mathscr{W}_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d},\varepsilon_{d})\simeq H^{d+1}_{B}(W_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d})\otimes H^{d+1}_{B}(W_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d}).

Combining this last with equations (
13
) we get

\displaystyle H^{2d+2}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d},\varepsilon_{d})

\displaystyle\simeq(S_{d+2}(N)\oplus\overline{S_{d+2}(N)})^{\otimes 2}

\displaystyle=(S_{d+2}(N)\otimes\overline{S_{d+2}(N)})\oplus(\overline{S_{d+2}(N)}\otimes S_{d+2}(N))

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\oplus(S_{d+2}(N)\otimes S_{d+2}(N))

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\oplus(\overline{S_{d+2}(N)}\otimes\overline{S_{d+2}(N)}).

In the last direct sum decomposition one can easily recognise the four graded pieces that constitute the cohomology group:

(14a)

\displaystyle H^{d+1,d+1}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d},\varepsilon_{d})

\displaystyle=(S_{d+2}(N)\otimes\overline{S_{d+2}(N)})\oplus(\overline{S_{d+2}(N)}\otimes S_{d+2}(N))

(14b)

\displaystyle H^{2d+2,0}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d},\varepsilon_{d})

\displaystyle=(S_{d+2}(N)\otimes S_{d+2}(N))

(14c)

\displaystyle H^{0,2d+2}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{d},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{d},\varepsilon_{d})

\displaystyle=(\overline{S_{d+2}(N)}\otimes\overline{S_{d+2}(N)}).

5
p
-adic
L
-functions

In this section we introduce the
p
-adic
L
-functions we will study. We regard them as functions on the weight space
\mathcal{W}
, i.e. the rigid analytic space whose
K
-points are
\mathcal{W}(K)=\Hom(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times},K^{\times})
for every extension
K
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Arithmetic weights are those of the form
\nu_{\vartheta,n}
for
\vartheta
a Dirichlet character of
p
-power conductor and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, acting via
\nu_{\vartheta,n}(z)=\vartheta(z)z^{n}
. We will often use the additive notation: the character
\nu_{\vartheta,n}
will be denoted with
\vartheta+n
.

Let
\mathcal{W}_{\pm}
denote the two halves of the weight space whose
M
-points are, for every extension
M/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
:

\mathcal{W}_{\pm}(M)=\{\kappa\in\Hom(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times},M^{\times})\mid\kappa(-1)=\pm 1\}.

In particular, for the arithmetic weights

\vartheta+n=\nu_{\vartheta,n}\in\mathcal{W}_{\pm}\iff\vartheta(-1)=\pm(-1)^{n}.

5.1
Kubota-Leopoldt and Symmetric square

The Kubota-Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function interpolates the values of a Dirichlet complex
L
-function at infinitely many negative integers. Let
\chi
be a Dirichlet character of conductor
N_{\chi}
, and write it as
\chi=\chi_{p}\chi^{\prime}
, where
\chi_{p}
has
p
-power conductor and
\chi^{\prime}
has conductor prime to
p
. Since
p
-power conductor characters may be incorporated into the weight, we can suppose
\chi_{p}=1
without loss of generality, i.e.
\chi
has conductor prime to
p
. Let
a_{\chi}
be
0
if
\chi
is even,
1
if it is odd. Denote the Gauss sum of a character with:

\tau(\chi)=\sum_{n=1}^{N_{\chi}}e^{\frac{2\pi in}{N_{\chi}}}\chi(n).

Proposition 5.1.1
.

There exists a unique
p
-adic meromorphic (holomorphic when
\chi
is not trivial) function
L_{p}(\chi,\cdot)
which at arithmetic weights
\vartheta+n
satisfies the interpolation property: if
n\leq 0
and
\chi\vartheta(-1)=(-1)^{n+1}
, then

\displaystyle L_{p}(\chi,\vartheta+n)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}_{p}(\chi\vartheta^{-1},n)L(\chi\vartheta^{-1},n)

while if
n\geq 1
and
\chi\vartheta(-1)=(-1)^{n}
, then

\displaystyle L_{p}(\chi,\vartheta+n)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}_{p}(\chi^{-1}\vartheta,1-n)\frac{2\Gamma(n)i^{a_{\chi}}}{(2\pi i)^{n}}\frac{\chi(N_{\vartheta})\tau(\vartheta^{-1})}{N_{\vartheta}^{n}}L(\chi\vartheta^{-1},n)

where
\mathrm{E}_{p}(\vartheta,x)=(1-\vartheta(p)p^{-x})
.

The set of points at which we have interpolation is dense in
\mathcal{W}
, hence
L_{p}(\chi,\cdot)
is well-defined. Notice that the choice of a “parity condition” on
\vartheta
and
n
is equivalent to choosing one half of the weight space. Indeed, the value of
\vartheta+n
at
-1
determines the parity of the integer
t
for which
(\vartheta+n)|_{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}=\omega^{t}
,
\omega
being the Teichmüller character. A priori one could choose two unrelated interpolation properties on the two halves of the weight space: following Dasgupta, we have made a choice that yields a functional equation.

Theorem 5.1.2
.

For every
\kappa\in\mathcal{W}
, we have

L_{p}(\chi,1-\kappa)=\frac{i^{a_{\chi}}}{\tau(\chi^{-1})}\kappa(N_{\chi})L_{p}(\chi^{-1},\kappa).

By contrast, the values of the symmetric square complex
L
-function
L(\Sym^{2}f)
have been interpolated in a
p
-adic
L
-function only when
f
is ordinary or
v_{p}(a_{p})<\tfrac{k+1}{2}
. Since we do not put restrictions on
v_{p}(a_{p})
we cannot take advantage of such a result. In particular, in our case the putative function
L_{p}(\Sym^{2}f)
is
not defined
. The construction of
L_{p}(\Sym^{2}f)
is one of the main goals of the paper.

5.1.1
The Euler System of cyclotomic classes

Let
U
be a
\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{\varphi(N_{\chi})}]
-module with an action of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Define the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space:

(15)

U^{\chi}=\{x\in U\mid\sigma(x)=(\chi)_{Gal}^{-1}(\sigma)x,\;\forall\sigma\in G_{\mathbb{Q}}\}.

By definition of
U^{\chi}
the absolute Galois group
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
acts through
(\chi)_{Gal}^{-1}
.

Consider in particular the case
U=\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}
for
F
a number field containing the
N_{\chi}
-th roots of unity, that is for an extension of
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N_{\chi}})
. Let
\zeta
be a primitive
N_{\chi}
-th root of unity, then the cyclotomic units are:

u_{\chi}=\prod_{1\leq a\leq N_{\chi}}(1-\zeta^{a})^{\chi^{-1}(a)}\in(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})^{\chi}.

We emphasise that the above units are by construction an “averaged over a twist” version of the elements

1-\zeta^{a}\in\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}\subseteq H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec F,1).

We have written
u_{\chi}
in multiplicative notation as we want to emphasise the identification
F^{\times}\simeq H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec F,1)
given by the Kummer map. One can also regard
u_{\chi}
as the image of an element of
\bigl(H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec F^{\prime},1)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(\chi)\bigr)^{\chi}
, where
F=F^{\prime}\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N_{\chi}})
. The above classes constitute of one of the earliest examples of Euler systems, and their étale realisations can be made into an Euler system in the sense of Rubin by taking care of the Euler factor in the
p
-direction, see
[
Rub00
,
\nopp
III]
. These collection of classes all arise as twists of a unique Euler system by finite-order characters. In particular, for any character of prime-to-
p
conductor, the twisted classes
u_{\chi}
give an Euler system for the representation
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1)\otimes\chi
, while the “original” classes are an Euler system for
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1)
. The shape of (the étale realisation of)
u_{\chi}
also comes from the general theory of twists of Euler systems by characters
[
Rub00
,
\nopp
II.4]
.

Accordingly, the
u_{\chi}
are linked to both complex and
p
-adic
L
-values. Since we will not use these formulæ directly, we only give them for
\chi
even for the sake of brevity. Write
\log_{\infty}
and
\log_{p}
for the complex and
p
-adic logarithm respectively. The following formulæ for
s=1
can be found in
[
Was82
]
, pages 37 and 63, while the ones for
s=0
follow from the functional equations.

Proposition 5.1.3
.

If
\chi
is even and non-trivial, then:

\displaystyle L(\chi^{-1},1)=-\frac{\tau(\chi^{-1})}{N_{\chi}}\log_{\infty}(u_{\chi}),

\displaystyle L^{\prime}(\chi,0)=-\log_{\infty}(u_{\chi}).

Theorem 5.1.4

(Leopoldt)
.

If
\chi
is even and non-trivial, then:

\displaystyle L_{p}(\chi^{-1},1)=-\frac{\tau(\chi^{-1})}{N_{\chi}}\Bigl(1-\frac{\chi^{-1}(p)}{p}\Bigr)\log_{p}(u_{\chi}),

\displaystyle L_{p}(\chi,0)=-i^{a_{\chi}}\Bigl(1-\frac{\chi^{-1}(p)}{p}\Bigr)\log_{p}(u_{\chi}).

Since we want to allow representations with higher twists, we have to search for analogues of
u_{\chi}
lying in
H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec F,n+1)
for any
n>0
. Here one can choose to appeal to motivic elements constructed by Beilinson
[
Beǐ84
]
or by Soulé
[
Sou84
]
.
Even though the two belong to slightly different cohomology groups (Soulé elements have coefficients in a integral lattice), they actually only differ by a rational factor. For further explanation on this, see
[
Gro94
]
,
[
Per95
, §3.2.4 and §5.1]
and
[
BK90
, 384]
for comparison of the elements in motivic cohomology and their regulator formulæ, and
[
HW98
,
HK03
]
which also treat the computations of the polylogarithms.

The following theorem is
[
Beǐ84
, Corollary 7.1.6]
, but the precise form of the regulator is to be found in the aforementioned references of Perrin-Riou and Bloch and Kato. The reader that wishes a formula in terms of the polylogarithm can consult the other references reported above.

Theorem 5.1.5

(Beilinson)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, then

\dim(H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec F,n+1)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(\chi))^{\chi}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\chi(-1)=(-1)^{n}$}\\
0&\text{if $\chi(-1)=(-1)^{n+1}$}\end{cases}.

There exists an element
\varphi_{\chi}\in(H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec F,n+1)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(\chi))^{\chi}
such that

r_{\mathcal{D}}(\varphi_{\chi})=-n!L(\overline{\chi},n+1).

According to the theorem, if
\chi(-1)=(-1)^{n}
then
H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec F,n+1)^{\chi}
is
1
-dimensional, otherwise it is zero dimensional, see also
[
Sou86
, §4.1]
. We have then a manifestation of the Beilinson conjecture—predicting the injectivity of
r_{\mathcal{D}}
—through the above equation. The parity condition is an indication that the Beilinson conjecture holds true for spectra of number fields. Using the functional equation for
L(\chi,\cdot)
and the computation of the residue of the Gamma function, we deduce

(16)

r_{\mathcal{D}}(\varphi_{\chi})=-n!L(\overline{\chi},n+1)=-\Bigl(\frac{2\pi i}{N_{\chi}}\Bigr)^{n}\tau(\chi)L^{\prime}(\chi,-n).

One also obtains the following formula relating
\varphi_{\chi}
to a
p
-adic
L
-value. Denote with
\log
the Bloch-Kato logarithm.

Theorem 5.1.6

(
[
Per95
, Proposition 3.2.3]
)
.

Under the hypothesis
\chi(-1)=(-1)^{n}
, the étale cohomology class
r_{\et}(\varphi_{\chi})\in H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec F,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1+n))^{\chi}
satisfies

\log\bigl(r_{\et}(\varphi_{\chi})\bigr)=\tau(\chi)\Bigl(1-\frac{\chi^{-1}(p)}{p^{1+n}}\Bigr)^{-1}(-1)^{n}n!L_{p}(\overline{\chi},1+n).

We warn the reader that we our convention differs from Perrin-Riou’s, especially regarding the eigenspaces for Galois characters. The eigenspace (
15
) is
U^{\chi}
in our notation, while in her notation it would be
U^{\chi^{-1}}
. For this reason we had to adjust Perrin-Riou’s proposition to our notation: this is why the above equation differs from the original one by a conjugation of the character. Using the functional equation for
L_{p}(\chi,\cdot)
, we deduce:

(17)

\log\bigl(r_{\et}(\varphi_{\chi})\bigr)=(-1)^{n}\frac{n!}{N_{\chi}^{n}}i^{a_{\chi}}\Bigl(1-\frac{\chi^{-1}(p)}{p^{n+1}}\Bigr)^{-1}L_{p}(\chi,-n).

Remark
.

If we let
n=k-j
, the condition under which
H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec F,1+k-j)^{\chi}
is non-trivial is
k-j\equiv a_{\chi}
modulo
2
. When
\chi=\psi
, this is exactly the condition we imposed at page
8
to deduce equation (
8
).

Integrality of higher cyclotomic classes

To end our account of higher cyclotomic classes we show that they are integral, which means that they are in
(H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{F},1+n)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(\chi))^{\chi}
. Other than being interesting on its own, this will allow for a more precise comparison with the motivic classes we will construct, as the cohomology groups of the ring of integers are smaller.

By definition, the motivic cohomology groups of spectra of number fields are

H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec F,1+n)=\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{gr}_{1+n}^{\gamma}K_{2n+1}(F).

In particular, motivic groups are torsion-free.

Suppose first
n\geq 1
. Then as
2n+1\geq 3
, the
K
-groups satisfy

K_{2n+1}(F)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}=K_{2n+1}(\mathcal{O}_{F})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}.

As all motivic classes are in the torsion-free part of the
K
-group, we can regard
\varphi_{\chi}\in(K_{2n+1}(\mathcal{O}_{F})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathbb{Q}(\chi)\simeq H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{F},n+1)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(\chi)
. Since
\varphi_{\chi}
is already in the
\chi
-eigenspace, the claim follows.

Suppose now
n=0
. In this case
\varphi_{\chi}\in(H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec F,1)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(\chi))^{\chi}\simeq(F^{\times}\otimes\mathbb{Q}(\chi))^{\chi}
. We compare it with the cyclotomic unit: thanks to the formulæ expressing
\varphi_{\chi}
and
u_{\chi}
in terms of
L
-values, we find

\varphi_{\chi}=-\frac{N_{\chi}}{\tau(\chi^{-1})}u_{\chi}.

Since
F
contains the
N_{\chi}
-th roots of unity, which are then in its ring of integers, we have
\tau(\chi^{-1})\in\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}\otimes\mathbb{Q}(\chi)
. Moreover
u_{\chi}\in(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})^{\chi}
as known. These imply that
\varphi_{\chi}\in\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}\otimes\mathbb{Q}(\chi)
, and since
\varphi_{\chi}
is already in the
\chi
-eigenspace, the claim follows.

5.2
Rankin-Selberg

The Rankin-Selberg
p
-adic
L
-function is usually constructed by interpolating critical values of the complex one. However, this definition is possible only when the weights of
f
and
g
are different, otherwise the lack of critical values undermines it. In this subsection we explain a workaround.

Recall that
L(f\otimes g,s)
denotes the complex Rankin-Selberg
L
-function attached to
f\in S_{k+2}(N,\psi)
and
g\in S_{k^{\prime}+2}(N^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
; without loss of generality suppose
k\geq k^{\prime}
. The construction of a
p
-adic
L
-function by interpolation—due to Panchishkin and Hida—relies on the existence of a “period” for
L(f\otimes g,s)
in the range
[k^{\prime}+2,k+1]

[
Shi77
]
. Hida then showed that for
f
ordinary there exists a unique
p
-adic
L
-function varying analytically as
f
varies in a Hida family, and with an interpolation property in the above range.

Nevertheless in this paper we are interested in the case
f=g
supersingular, so we are violating both requirements of that construction. In particular, the value one would like to call
L_{p}(f,f,s)
is never obtained by interpolation, but by evaluating a
unique
function on the weight space (with constraints given elsewhere) at appropriately chosen weight-characters. This also entails that the factorisation formula cannot be obtained by interpolation, contrarily to what the naive approach would suggest. Furthermore, being in the supersingular case we replace Hida families of modular forms with Coleman families.

We do not aim to give here a full-fledged account of the topic of families of modular forms, we only explain the results relevant to our case, following
[
LZ16
]
. The reader who wishes to know more on the subject can consult the seminal work of Coleman and Mazur
[
CM98
]
, and the articles
[
Col97
,
Buz07
,
Eme11
]
.

If
U\subseteq\mathcal{W}
is an affinoid defined over a finite extension
H
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, denote with
\Lambda_{U}
the
\mathcal{O}_{H}
-algebra of rigid functions on
U
bounded by
1
. This is isomorphic to the
\mathcal{O}_{H}
-algebra of formal power series in one variable,
\Lambda_{U}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{H}[[T]]
. In the remainder of this subsection,
f
is supposed to be supersingular.

Definition 5.2.1
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathcal{W}
be an affinoid disc defined over a finite extension
H
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, such that the set of classical weights
U\cap\mathbb{N}
is dense in
U
. A
Coleman family

F
over
U
(of tame level
N
) is a power series

F=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}(F)q^{n}\in\Lambda_{U}[[q]]

with
a_{1}(F)=1
and
a_{p}(F)
invertible in
\Lambda_{U}[1/p]
, such that for all but finitely many classical weights
k_{0}\in U\cap\mathbb{N}
, the series
F_{k_{0}}=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}(F)(k_{0})q^{n}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{O}_{H}
is the
q
-expansion of a classical modular form of weight
k_{0}+2
and level
\Gamma_{1}(N)\cap\Gamma_{0}(p)
which is a normalised eigenform.

Roughly speaking, a Coleman family is a formal
q
-expansion with coefficients in
\Lambda_{U}
, which can be specialised to every weight-character in
U
, to obtain a modular form of that weight-character. The condition on
a_{p}(F)
implies that it is never zero, hence all specialisations have finite slope.

If
\breve{f}
is a modular form of weight
\breve{k}+2\geq 2
and level
\Gamma_{1}(N)\cap\Gamma_{0}(p)
, a Coleman family
passing through

\breve{f}
is a family
F
over
U
such that
\breve{k}\in U
and
F_{\breve{k}}=\breve{f}
. Since
U
is an affinoid disc, the tame level and tame character are constant along the family.

The family
F
does not need to exist in general, but it is guaranteed to do so if
\breve{f}
is a
noble
eigenform, i.e. if it is a normalised cuspidal Hecke eigenform such that

•

it is the
p
-stabilisation of a newform whose Satake parameters at
p
are distinct (“
p
-regularity”);

•

if
v_{p}(a_{p}(\breve{f}))=\breve{k}+1
, then the Galois representation
M_{\etbar}(f)|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
is not the direct sum of two characters (“non-criticality”).

Theorem 5.2.2

(
[
LZ16
, Theorem 4.6.4]
)
.

Suppose
\breve{f}
is a noble eigenform of weight
\breve{k}+2
, then there exists a disc
U\subseteq\mathcal{W}
with
\breve{k}\in U
and a unique Coleman family
F
over
U
, such that
F_{\breve{k}}=\breve{f}
.

Coleman families are the key ingredient to construct
p
-adic
L
-functions for families of supersingular forms.

Theorem 5.2.3

(
[
LZ16
]
)
.

Let
f,g
be cusp forms of level
\Gamma_{1}(N)
which are supersingular at
p
, with chosen
p
-stabilisations
f_{\alpha_{f}}
,
g_{\alpha_{g}}
. Suppose that
N\geq 5
and that
f_{\alpha_{f}}
and
g_{\alpha_{g}}
are noble. Let
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
be affinoid discs around
k
and
k^{\prime}
respectively,
F
and
G
Coleman families over
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
respectively, passing through
f_{\alpha_{f}}
and
g_{\alpha_{g}}
. Then there exists a unique meromorphic rigid function

\displaystyle L_{p}^{\mathrm{geom}}(F,G)\colon V_{1}\times V_{2}\times\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C}_{p}

\displaystyle(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\upsilon)

\displaystyle\mapsto L_{p}^{\mathrm{geom}}(F,G)(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\upsilon)

with the following interpolation property. Suppose that

•

(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\upsilon)=(k_{0},k^{\prime}_{0},j_{0})
for integers
k_{0}\geq 0
,
k^{\prime}_{0}\geq-1
and
k^{\prime}_{0}+1\leq j_{0}\leq k_{0}
;

•

f_{k_{0},\alpha}=F_{k_{0}}
and
g_{k^{\prime}_{0},\alpha}=G_{k^{\prime}_{0}}
are the
p
-stabilisations of forms
f_{k_{0}},g_{k^{\prime}_{0}}
.

Then

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}L_{p}^{\mathrm{geom}}(F,G)(k_{0},k^{\prime}_{0},j_{0}+1)=\frac{j_{0}!(j_{0}-k^{\prime}_{0}-1)!}{\pi^{2j_{0}-k^{\prime}_{0}+1}(-i)^{k_{0}-k^{\prime}_{0}}2^{2j_{0}+2+k_{0}-k^{\prime}_{0}}\langle f_{k_{0}},f_{k_{0}}\rangle}\\
\cdot\frac{E(f_{k_{0}},g_{k^{\prime}_{0}},j_{0}+1)}{E(f_{k_{0}})E^{*}(f_{k_{0}})}L(f_{k_{0}},g_{k^{\prime}_{0}},j_{0}+1)\end{multlined}

where

\displaystyle E(f)=\Bigl(1-\frac{\beta_{f}}{p\alpha_{f}}\Bigr),\quad E^{*}(f)=\Bigl(1-\frac{\beta_{f}}{\alpha_{f}}\Bigr),

\displaystyle E(f,g,j+1)=\Bigl(1-\frac{p^{j}}{\alpha_{f}\alpha_{g}}\Bigr)\Bigl(1-\frac{p^{j}}{\alpha_{f}\beta_{g}}\Bigr)\Bigl(1-\frac{\beta_{f}\alpha_{g}}{p^{1+j}}\Bigr)\Bigl(1-\frac{\beta_{f}\beta_{g}}{p^{1+j}}\Bigr).

The function defined in the theorem is a
p
-adic
L
-function in three variables, where we have analytic variation as the two modular forms vary in Coleman families, and the last variable over a subset of
\mathcal{W}
. For every specialisation of
F
and
G
we obtain a single-variable
p
-adic
L
-function. The
3
-variable function was initially constructed in the main theorem of
[
Urb14
, §4.4]
, but the article had a gap which was addressed in
[
AI17
]
. In the meanwhile, Zerbes and the second author gave an alternative proof, which is the one we refer to.

If we start from the supersingular eigenforms
f
and
g
, then their
p
-stabilisations automatically satisfy the second requirement. It suffices then to ask that
f
and
g
have distinct Satake parameters to ensure that there exist unique Coleman families passing through their
p
-stabilisations. In this case we can then apply the last theorem to deduce the existence of the geometric
p
-adic
L
-function. For this reason, from now on we will make the following assumption:

•

the supersingular form
f
satisfies
\alpha_{f}\neq\beta_{f}
.

Both
p
-stabilisations of
f
are then noble, so we fix one of them and deduce the existence of a unique Coleman family
F
through it.

Remark
.

Clearly the condition
\alpha_{f}\neq\beta_{f}
is equivalent to the fact that the Hecke operator
T_{p}
acts semisimply on
M_{\et}(f)
. Coleman and Edixhoven
[
CE98
]
showed that this is always the case in weight
2
, and that it is implied by Tate’s conjecture in higher weights. Therefore, the case
\alpha_{f}=\beta_{f}
never occurs when
k=0
, and is conjectured to never occur also when
k>0
.

Note that
L_{p}^{\mathrm{geom}}(F,F)
is always well-defined: there is no requirement about the weights of the two cusp forms. There are no interpolation results when we specialise the Coleman families at the same arithmetic weights, but we can still speak of the value
L_{p}^{\mathrm{geom}}(F,F)(k,k,\upsilon)
for every
\upsilon
. The crucial point is that these values are
not
related to
L(f,f,s)
for any value of
s
, since the “interpolation condition” is not fulfilled. As written above, this is the actual reason why we cannot prove the
p
-adic factorisation theorem simply interpolating the complex
L
-functions. In the remainder of the article we will use
L_{p}^{\mathrm{geom}}(F,F)
as a replacement for the missing Rankin-Selberg
p
-adic
L
-function. Indeed, since it interpolates a range of complex Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions, it can be considered a generalisation of the
p
-adic one.

Remark
.

In the literature there are a few different versions of the interpolation formula for the geometric
p
-adic
L
-function. The above formula agrees with the conventions of
[
BH20
]
, which in turn draws from
[
Urb14
,
AI17
]
. The version stated in
[
LZ16
]
is slightly different: firstly, it relates
L(f_{k_{0}},g_{k^{\prime}_{0}},j_{0}+1)
to the value of
L_{p}^{\mathrm{geom}}(F,G)
at
(k_{0},k^{\prime}_{0},j_{0})
instead of at
(k_{0},k^{\prime}_{0},j_{0}+1)
. Secondly, the factor
(-i)^{k_{0}-k^{\prime}_{0}}
is replaced with
(-1)^{k_{0}-k^{\prime}_{0}}
. We underline that we will need this function only in the case where the two cusp forms coincide, therefore this last difference disappears. In that case, passing from one convention to the other is just a matter of translating the last variable by
\pm 1
, so the two versions actually define completely interchangeable
p
-adic functions.

Examples of non-noble modular forms

We give here examples of how one can construct non-noble modular forms. In these cases it is not guaranteed that the modular form can be put in a family (this corresponds to the fact that the eigencurve is not necessarily étale over the weight space). Noble forms have level
\Gamma_{1}(N)\cap\Gamma_{0}(p)
, so we work with a form
\breve{f}
of weight
\breve{k}+2\geq 2
and level
\Gamma_{1}(N)\cap\Gamma_{0}(p)
. In addition, noble forms are the
p
-stabilisations of cusp forms with distinct Satake parameters. When
\breve{f}
is not the
p
-stabilisation of a form, it is automatically non-noble. Nevertheless, the most interesting case is that of forms which arise as
p
-stabilisations, so we put ourselves in this case.

Let then
\breve{f}
be a modular form of weight
\breve{k}+2
and level
\Gamma_{1}(N)\cap\Gamma_{0}(p)
, which is the
p
-stabilisation of a cusp form
h
of level
\Gamma_{1}(N)
. Notice that this implies that
\breve{f}
is old at
p
.

Nobility amounts to two features:
p
-regularity and non-criticality. If we assume the Coleman-Edixhoven conjecture, all cusp forms have distinct Satake parameters, hence
p
-regularity is always satisfied.

For
\breve{f}
to be noble, it must also be non-critical. We say that
\breve{f}
has
critical slope
if
v_{p}(a_{p}(\breve{f}))=\breve{k}+1
, and that it is
critical
if it has critical slope, and
M_{\etbar}(\breve{f})|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
splits as the direct sum of two characters. This amounts to say that
M_{\etbar}(\breve{f})
is semisimple as a
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-representation.

By the Coleman-Edixhoven conjecture, if
v_{p}(a_{p}(\breve{f}))\neq\breve{k}+1
then
\breve{f}
is automatically noble. Non-noble forms can only be found when
\breve{f}
has critical slope, and in addition the form is critical. We now apply a result of Bellaïche which characterises forms with critical slope.

Proposition 5.2.4

(
[
Bel12
, Proposition 2.13 and Remark 2.14]
)
.

Let
h
be a newform. Then there exists a
p
-stabilisation of
h
of critical slope in and only in the following cases:

•

h
is cuspidal,
p
-ordinary and not CM. In this case it is expected that
\breve{f}
is non-critical;

•

h
is cuspidal, with CM by an imaginary quadratic field where
p
is split. In this case
\breve{f}
is always critical;

•

h
is Eisenstein. In this case it is expected that
\breve{f}
is non-critical.

In these cases there exists exactly one
p
-stabilisation of critical slope.

Therefore, non-criticality is violated only in the second case of the proposition. This provides us with an infinite supply of non-noble forms:
p
-stabilisations of cusp forms with CM by an imaginary quadratic field where
p
splits. On the other hand, in all the other cases the resulting forms are of critical slope, but not critical. In particular,
p
-stabilisations of cusp forms without CM are expected to always be noble.

We now give an explicit example of a non-noble modular form, arising as
p
-stabilisation of cuspidal forms with CM, with
p
split in the imaginary quadratic field. Consider the form with label 27.2.a.a in the
L
-functions and Modular Forms Database
[
LMFDB
,
Modular Form 27.2.a.a
]
. This is a modular form of level
\Gamma_{0}(27)
and weight
2
, with CM by
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3})
. Its
q
-expansion is

h(q)=q-2q^{4}-q^{7}+5q^{13}+4q^{16}-7q^{19}+O(q^{20}).

Let
p=7
, then
a_{7}(h)=-1
so it is a
7
-adic unit. The form
h
is then cuspidal, ordinary and with CM by
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3})
, and in this field the prime
7
splits as
(7)=(2-\sqrt{-3})(2+\sqrt{-3})
. We are then in the second case above. It follows that one of the two
p
-stabilisations of
h
is critical. Since critical implies critical slope, the correct candidate is the
p
-stabilisation corresponding to the root of
7
-adic valuation
+1
. This is a non-noble modular form which arises as a
p
-stabilisation of a cusp form.

6
The Beilinson-Flach Euler system

In this section we define the cohomology classes that we will use as ingredients to construct our motivic classes. We first introduce Beilinson-Flach classes and explain how they can be extended to the cohomology of Kuga-Sato varieties, and then exploit some differential forms attached to cusp forms.

6.1
From Eisenstein to Beilinson-Flach classes

In this subsection we introduce the Beilinson-Flach classes, constructed in
[
LLZ14
,
KLZ15
]
as the push forward of Eisenstein classes (constructed by Beilinson) from the motivic cohomology of modular curves, to that of the product of two modular curves.

Definition 6.1.1

(
[
KLZ15
, Definition 5.3.1]
)
.

The Beilinson-Flach classes defined over
\mathbb{Q}
are motivic classes

\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\mot,N}\in H^{3}_{\mot}(Y_{1}(N)^{2},\TSym^{[k,k^{\prime}]}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(2-j)).

We will suppress the subscript
N
, since it is constant in the paper. For any theory
\mathcal{T}\in\{\et,\dR,\mathrm{Betti},\mathcal{D},\syn,\rig\}
denote
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\mathcal{T}}=r_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\mot})
.

The key feature of Beilinson-Flach classes is that they provide an Euler system for the Galois representation attached to the convolution of
f
and
g
. For us the most interesting feature is their relation to the special values of
L^{\prime}(f,g)
via a regulator formula: the bridge is the regulator to Deligne cohomology
r_{\mathcal{D}}
. For our purposes it is also necessary to extend Beilinson-Flach classes to
X_{1}(N)^{2}
while keeping a similar formula. The “compactification” process is done in three steps:

•

construct cohomology classes over
X_{1}(N)^{2}
extending Beilinson-Flach classes (Subsection
6.2
);

•

construct an appropriate differential form to pair them with (Subsection
6.4
);

•

prove a version of the regulator formula over
X_{1}(N)^{2}
(Subsubsection
7.1.1
).

Remark
.

The map
r_{\mathcal{D}}
can be expressed explicitly as an integral when one starts from
H_{\mot}^{3}(X,2)\simeq\mathrm{CH}^{2}(X,1)
with
X
a proper variety. Recall that the higher Chow group
\mathrm{CH}^{i+1}(X,1)
can be described as the cohomology in the middle place of the Gersten complex

\bigoplus_{y\in Z^{i-1}}K_{2}(k(x))\xrightarrow{T}\bigoplus_{x\in Z^{i}}k(x)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{div}}\bigoplus_{Z^{i+1}}\mathbb{Z}

Hence every element of the motivic cohomology group
H^{3}_{\mot}(X,2)
has also a description as an element of
\mathrm{CH}^{2}(X,1)
, i.e. as a sum
\sum_{j}(Z_{j},f_{j})
where
Z_{j}\subseteq X
are subvarieties of codimension
1
, and
f_{j}
are functions such that
\sum_{j}\mathrm{div}(f_{j})=0
. In this case the regulator can be written as:

\displaystyle r_{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathrm{CH}^{i}(X,1)

\displaystyle\to H^{2d-i,2d-i}_{\dR}(X,\mathbb{C})^{\vee}\simeq H^{i,i}_{\dR}(X,\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle\sum_{j}(Z_{j},f_{j})

\displaystyle\mapsto\Bigl(\omega\mapsto\sum_{j}\int_{Z_{j}}\log|f_{j}|\omega\Bigr).

Notice the similarity with Definition
3.1.3
: as we suggested, there is a tight relation between
r_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\cl_{\dR}
which we will investigate in
7.1
. Specialise to the case
X=X_{1}(N)^{2}
. When
k=l=0
,
j=0
we end up with Beilinson-Flach classes lying in
H^{3}_{\mot}(X_{1}(N)^{2},2)\simeq\mathrm{CH}^{2}(X_{1}(N),1)
, which corresponds to the case
i=2
above. However, aside from this special case we cannot interpret the motivic cohomology groups as Chow groups, because the coefficients are not trivial. We can overcome this issue employing the results of Section
4
and working with motivic cohomology of Kuga-Sato varieties: since in this case the coefficients are always trivial, we can rely on the Chow groups interpretation and fall back on geometric techniques to prove our claims.

6.2
Compactification of Beilinson-Flach classes

In this subsection we explain how to pass from Beilinson-Flach classes defined over
Y_{1}(N)^{2}
(equivalently, over
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
) to a version defined over
X_{1}(N)^{2}
(equivalently, over
\mathscr{W}_{k}
). The key difference is that
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
is not proper, while
\mathscr{W}_{k}
is both proper and smooth. Indeed, the motivation for this entire process is to place ourselves in a position of using the full force of cohomology, in particular Hodge theory. The process goes under the name
compactification of motivic classes
.

The idea is to find an element defined over
\mathscr{W}_{k}
that pulls back to the Beilinson-Flach one. The defect should be supported only on the cuspidal locus
\mathscr{W}_{k}^{\infty}
. In other words, we are searching for an element which

•

makes Beilinson-Flach classes defined over
\mathscr{W}_{k}
;

•

is defined over
\mathscr{W}_{k}^{\infty}
, i.e. is
negligible
.

This task is carried out in
[
BC16
, §7-8]
, which we now explain. Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{k,k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\;\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,k^{\prime},*}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,*}\times\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k^{\prime},*},

\displaystyle Z^{k}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,*}\setminus\mathcal{E}^{k}=\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,*}\times_{X_{1}(N)}X_{1}(N)^{\infty},

\displaystyle\;Z^{k,k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=Z^{k}\times Z^{k^{\prime}},

\displaystyle U^{k,k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,k^{\prime},*}\setminus Z^{k,k^{\prime}}.

One should think of
Z^{k}
as the smooth cuspidal locus (i.e. the fibre over the cusps) and
U^{k,k^{\prime}}
as the “open” locus. By definition
U^{k}=\mathcal{E}^{k}
at least set-theoretically, but
U^{k,k^{\prime}}
is strictly larger than
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
. For example, it contains both
Z^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times Z^{k^{\prime}}
. We naturally have

\mathcal{E}^{k+k^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow U^{k,k^{\prime}}.

Brunault and Chida define lifted classes
\widetilde{\mathrm{BF}}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\mot}
as the push-forward of Eisenstein classes to the cohomology of
U^{k,k^{\prime}}
rather than of
\mathcal{E}^{k,k^{\prime}}
. The lifted classes then live in
H^{3+k+k^{\prime}}_{\mot}(U^{k,k^{\prime}},2+k+k^{\prime}-j)
and pull-back to the standard ones. The localisation sequence
Z^{k,k^{\prime}}\to\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,k^{\prime},*}\to U^{k,k^{\prime}}
induces a long exact sequence

\cdots\to H^{3+k+k^{\prime}}_{\mot}(\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,k^{\prime},*},2+k+k^{\prime}-j)(\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k^{\prime}})\\
\to H^{3+k+k^{\prime}}_{\mot}(U^{k,k^{\prime}},2+k+k^{\prime}-j)(\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k^{\prime}})\\
\xrightarrow{\Res}H^{k+k^{\prime}}_{\mot}(Z^{k,k^{\prime}},k+k^{\prime}-j)(\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k^{\prime}})\to\cdots

The residue
\Res(\widetilde{\mathrm{BF}}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\mot})
is non-zero only when
j=0
. On one hand, for
j>0
by exactness the lifted class has a preimage in the first group. On the other hand, when
j=0
one deduces the same thesis by diagram chasing: the “cuspidal embedding”
i_{\mathrm{cusp}}\colon Z^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow U^{k,k^{\prime}}
induces a push-forward morphism

H^{1+k+k^{\prime}}_{\mot}(Z^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}},1+k+k^{\prime})\xrightarrow{i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}}H^{3+k+k^{\prime}}_{\mot}(U^{k,k^{\prime}},2+k+k^{\prime}).

It turns out that there exists an element
\xi_{\beta}\in H^{k+k^{\prime}+1}_{\mot}(Z^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}},k+k^{\prime}+1)(\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k^{\prime}})
satisfying
\Res\circ i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta})=\Res(\widetilde{\mathrm{BF}}^{[k,k^{\prime},0]}_{\mot})
. This shows that the element

(
\sharp
)

\begin{cases}\widetilde{\mathrm{BF}}^{[k,k^{\prime},0]}_{\mot}-i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta})&\text{if $j=0$}\\
\widetilde{\mathrm{BF}}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\mot}&\text{if $j>0$}\end{cases}

has residue always equal to zero. We then denote with
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}
an arbitrary choice of a preimage of this element in
H^{3+k+k^{\prime}}_{\mot}(\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,k^{\prime},*},2+k+k^{\prime}-j)(\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k^{\prime}})
. Recall now that Theorem
4.1.3
establishes the existence of an isomorphism

H_{\mot}^{3+k+k^{\prime}}(W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}},\mathbb{Q}(j))(\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k^{\prime}})\simeq H_{\mot}^{3+k+k^{\prime}}(\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,*}\times\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k^{\prime},*},\mathbb{Q}(j))(\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k^{\prime}})

which shows that
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}
lifts to
W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}}
, which is
\mathscr{W}_{k}
when
k=k^{\prime}
. Reversing the argument shows that
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}
pulls back to
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\mot}
. Indeed, its pull back from
\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,k^{\prime},*}
to
\mathcal{E}^{k,k^{\prime}}
factors through
U^{k,k^{\prime}}
. But by definition, its image in the cohomology of
U^{k,k^{\prime}}
is the class (

♯

‣
6.2
), which clearly pulls back to
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\mot}
as the cuspidal contribution maps to zero.

Hence
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}
is a lift of
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\mot}
in the cohomology of
W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}}
, as wanted. These motivic classes are the
first version
of compactified Beilinson-Flach classes, as defined by Brunault and Chida. For any cohomology theory
\mathcal{T}\in\{\et,\dR,\mathrm{Betti},\mathcal{D},\syn,\rig\}
denote
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}_{\mathcal{T}}=r_{\mathcal{T}}(\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j})
.

Remark
.

Notice that the defect between
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\mot}
and
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}
is supported purely on
Z^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
. Therefore, its pairing with differential forms attached to cusp forms vanishes. Hence, as long as we stick to cusp forms, the classes
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}
and
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\mot}
satisfy the same regulator formulæ. A precise statement is given in
[
BC16
, Proposition 8.3]
, which we will recall later.

We now define a second version of compactified Beilinson-Flach classes for the coincident weights case. Since the kernel of the map induced by
\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,k^{\prime},*}\to U^{k,k^{\prime}}
is not guaranteed to be trivial, the class
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}
is not uniquely determined in general. Suppose now
k=k^{\prime}
and let
\rho^{\prime}
be the involution of
\mathscr{W}_{k}=W_{k}\times W_{k}
swapping the two components of the fibre product.

Definition 6.2.1
.

The
second version
of the motivic compactified Beilinson-Flach classes is

\Xi^{k,k,j}=\frac{1}{2}(\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}+(-1)^{k+j}(\rho^{\prime})^{*}(\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}))\in H^{3+2k}_{\mot}(\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,k,*},2+2k-j)(\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k}).

By the same argument as before, we regard
\Xi^{k,k,j}\in H^{3+2k}_{\mot}(\mathscr{W}_{k},2+2k-j)(\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{k})
. For any cohomology theory
\mathcal{T}\in\{\et,\dR,\mathrm{Betti},\mathcal{D},\syn,\rig\}
denote
\Xi^{k,k^{\prime},j}_{\mathcal{T}}=r_{\mathcal{T}}(\Xi^{k,k^{\prime},j})
.

The element
\Xi^{k,k,j}
is still a lifting of the non-compactified classes: its pull-back to
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
coincides with

\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}+(-1)^{k+j}(\rho^{\prime})^{*}\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot})=\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}+\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot})=\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}

where we have used the properties of non-compactified classes, see Subsubsection
6.3.3
.
\Xi^{k,k,j}
also satisfies an extra useful property: by definition, the involution
\rho^{\prime}
acts on
\Xi^{k,k,j}
with eigenvalue
(-1)^{k+j}
.

6.3
Classes in the cohomology of
f

In this subsection we explain how it is possible to pass from Beilinson-Flach classes associated to the triple
(k,k^{\prime},j)
to classes associated to the triple
(f,g,j)
. In the sequel we will mainly be interested in the case
f=g
. It is important to underline that the arguments of this subsection apply only to a restricted choice of cohomology theories, in particular they do
not
apply to motivic classes.

6.3.1
Classes in Deligne cohomology

In Deligne cohomology we have classes
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}_{\mathcal{D}}
. Following Subsection
3.1
there is an isomorphism

H^{3+k+k^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{D}}(W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}},K_{f,g}(2+k+k^{\prime}-j))\\
\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f,g}}}(K_{f,g},H^{2+k+k^{\prime}}_{B}(W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}},K_{f,g}(2+k+k^{\prime}-j)))

which we compose with the functorial morphism induced by
\pr_{f,g}

\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f,g}}}(K_{f,g},H^{2+k+k^{\prime}}_{B}(W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}},K_{f,g}(2+k+k^{\prime}-j)))\\
\xrightarrow{\pr_{f,g}}\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f,g}}}(K_{f,g},M_{B}(f\otimes g)^{*}(-j)).

We define
\widetilde{\Xi}^{f,g,j}_{\mathcal{D}}
as the image of
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}_{\mathcal{D}}
under this composition. When
k=k^{\prime}
we define
\Xi^{f,g,j}_{\mathcal{D}}
as the image of
\Xi^{k,k,j}_{\mathcal{D}}
under the same composition.

The first isomorphism still exists when we replace
W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}}
with
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
. This follows from the isomorphism expressing the cohomology of
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
as the cohomology of
Y_{1}(N)^{2}
, which is affine, and by Proposition
3.1.2
. Indeed,
Y_{1}(N)^{2}
has dimension
2
, hence its third Betti cohomology group is trivial, and obviously does not admit non-zero morphisms from the trivial structure. Furthermore, the morphism
\pr_{f,g}
is defined from the
\Ext
-groups with coefficients in the cohomology of
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
too. Hence the whole composition is well-defined even when starting from the Deligne cohomology of
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
. We then define
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,g,j]}_{\mathcal{D}}
as the image
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\mathcal{D}}
under it.

6.3.2
Classes in étale cohomology

In étale cohomology we have classes
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}_{\et}
. Following Subsection
3.2
there is a morphism

H^{3+k+k^{\prime}}_{\et}(W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2+k+k^{\prime}-j))\\
\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),H^{2+k+k^{\prime}}_{\et}((W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2+k+k^{\prime}-j)))

which we compose with the functorial morphism induced by
\pr_{f,g}

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),H^{2+k+k^{\prime}}_{\et}((W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2+k+k^{\prime}-j)))\\
\xrightarrow{\pr_{f,g}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes g)^{*}(-j)).

We define
\widetilde{\Xi}^{f,g,j}_{\et}
as the image of
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}_{\et}
under this composition. When
k=k^{\prime}
we define
\Xi^{f,g,j}_{\et}
as the image of
\Xi^{k,k,j}_{\et}
under the same composition.

The first morphism still exists when we replace
W_{k}\times W_{k^{\prime}}
with
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
. As before, this follows from the identification of the cohomology of
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
and of
Y_{1}(N)^{2}
, which is affine, and by Proposition
3.2.2
(again because the third cohomology group of the
2
-dimensional affine scheme
Y_{1}(N)^{2}
is trivial when we base change to
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
). Furthermore, the morphisms
\pr_{f,g}
is defined from the Galois cohomology groups with coefficients in the cohomology of
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
too. Hence the whole composition is well-defined also when starting from the étale cohomology of
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k^{\prime}}
. We then define
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,g,j]}_{\et}
as the image of
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k^{\prime},j]}_{\et}
under it.

From the above discussion, when
f=g
we have three different classes in the cohomology of
M_{\bullet}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-j)
for
\bullet\in\{B,\etbar\}
. Explicitly

\displaystyle\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\mathcal{D}},\;\widetilde{\Xi}^{f,f,j}_{\mathcal{D}},\;\Xi^{f,f,j}_{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle\in\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}(K_{f},M_{B}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-j)),

\displaystyle\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\et},\;\widetilde{\Xi}^{f,f,j}_{\et},\;\Xi^{f,f,j}_{\et}

\displaystyle\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes g)^{*}(-j)).

We record here a useful result which we will need in Subsection
8.1
.

Proposition 6.3.1
.

The classes
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,k]}_{\et}
,
\widetilde{\Xi}^{f,f,k}_{\et}
and
\Xi^{f,f,k}_{\et}
coincide.

Proof.

We show first that
\Xi^{f,f,j}_{\et}
coincides with
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\et}
. Let
\iota\colon\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{W}_{k}
. The pull-back of
\Xi^{k,k,j}_{\et}
under
\iota
coincides with
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\et}
. The morphism
\iota
also induces a morphism

\displaystyle H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},H^{2k+2}_{\et}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2+2k-j)))\xrightarrow{\iota^{*}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},H^{2k+2}_{\et}(\mathcal{E}^{k,k}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2+2k-j)))

which further induces

\displaystyle H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-j))\xrightarrow{\iota^{*}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-j)).

This last morphism is actually an isomorphism as explained in
4.2
. The images of
\Xi^{k,k,j}_{\et}
and
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\et}
in this last cohomology group are
\Xi^{f,f,j}_{\et}
and
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\et}
respectively. Since
\iota^{*}\Xi^{k,k,j}_{\et}=\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\et}
and the morphisms to
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-j))
commute with
\iota^{*}
, we obtain that
\iota^{*}\Xi^{f,f,j}_{\et}=\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\et}
. But
\iota^{*}
is now an isomorphism of this last group, so the two cohomology classes coincide.

The argument goes through unchanged if we replace
\Xi^{\bullet}_{\et}
with
\widetilde{\Xi}^{\bullet}_{\et}
, thus showing that
\widetilde{\Xi}^{f,f,j}_{\et}=\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\et}
. This proves the claim that all three classes coincide.
∎

The proof goes through
mutatis mutandis
for the case of the classes in the cohomology of
M_{B}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-j)
, because this last group has canonical isomorphisms to the cohomologies of
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
and
\mathscr{W}_{k}
too. Therefore, the classes
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,k]}_{\mathcal{D}}
,
\widetilde{\Xi}^{f,f,k}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\Xi^{f,f,k}_{\mathcal{D}}
also coincide.

6.3.3
Properties under swapping involutions

We collect here some properties regarding the behaviour of both compactified and non-compactified Beilinson-Flach classes under three different involutions, when
f=g
.

First of all, non-compactified classes belong to

H^{3+2k}_{\mot}(\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k},2+2k-j)\simeq H^{3}_{\mot}(Y_{1}(N)^{2},\TSym^{[k,k]}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(2-j)).

This cohomology group enjoys two different involutions. Let
\rho^{\prime}
and
\rho
be the involutions swapping the components of
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
and of
Y_{1}(N)^{2}
respectively. Since
\mathcal{E}^{k}
is
k
-dimensional over
Y_{1}(N)
we have
(\rho^{\prime})^{*}=(-1)^{k}\rho^{*}
. Hence the
(\pm 1)
-eigenspaces of
H^{3}_{\mot}(Y_{1}(N)^{2},\TSym^{[k,k]}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(2-j))
correspond to the
\pm(-1)^{k}
-eigenspaces of
H^{3+2k}_{\mot}(\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k},2+2k-j)
. According to
[
KLZ17
, Proposition 5.2.3]
the non-compactified classes satisfy

\displaystyle\rho^{*}\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}=(-1)^{j}\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot},

\displaystyle(\rho^{\prime})^{*}\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}=(-1)^{k+j}\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}.

Furthermore, for
\mathcal{T}\in\{\mathcal{D},\et\}
the classes
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\mathcal{T}}
are in cohomology groups with coefficients in
M_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-j)
for
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\in\{B,\etbar\}
respectively. We can then consider a third involution on these groups, induced by the involution
s
swapping the two components of the tensor product, see Subsection
4.2
. Notice that
s
is purely algebraic, it does not detect any geometric structure. Since the Künneth isomorphism is induced by the cup-product, which is commutative or alternating depending on the degree, the involution
s
differs from
\rho^{*}
and
(\rho^{\prime})^{*}
by a sign that also depends only on the degree.

In particular, the coefficients of
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\mathcal{T}}
are in cohomology groups of degrees
2k+2
or
2
according to the chosen interpretation. This means that we are taking tensor products of classes in degrees
k+1
and
1
respectively. Hence these classes satisfy:

s(\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\mathcal{T}})=-\rho^{*}\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\mathcal{T}}=(-1)^{k+1}(\rho^{\prime})^{*}\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\mathcal{T}}=(-1)^{j+1}\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\mathcal{T}}.

Therefore, by controlling the parity of
j
we are choosing whether Beilinson-Flach classes belong to cohomology with coefficients in
\Sym^{2}M_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}(f)^{*}
or in
\wedge^{2}M_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}(f)^{*}
.

Regarding compactified classes, we cannot say anything about the classes
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}
since they are chosen arbitrarily. We can still say something about their image in the cohomology of
U^{k,k^{\prime}}
though. The class
\widetilde{\mathrm{BF}}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}
pulls back to
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}
, so it has the same behaviour under the considered involutions. However, when
j=0
the extra contribution
i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta})
does not respect any symmetry property. It is actually defined asymmetrically with respect to the fibre product from the very beginning.

The second version of compactified classes
\Xi^{k,k,j}
has a much easier behaviour, which is also the reason for their introduction: by construction

\displaystyle(\rho^{\prime})^{*}\Xi^{k,k,j}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k+j}\Xi^{k,k,j},

\displaystyle\rho^{*}\Xi^{k,k,j}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}(-1)^{k+j}\Xi^{k,k,j}=(-1)^{j}\Xi^{k,k,j}.

Moreover, analogously to above we have

s(\Xi^{f,f,j}_{\mathcal{T}})=-\rho^{*}\Xi^{f,f,j}_{\mathcal{T}}=(-1)^{k+1}(\rho^{\prime})^{*}\Xi^{f,f,j}_{\mathcal{T}}=(-1)^{j+1}\Xi^{f,f,j}_{\mathcal{T}}.

The parity of
j
then controls also whether
\Xi^{k,k,j}
belongs to cohomology with coefficients in
\Sym^{2}M_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}(f)^{*}
or
\wedge^{2}M_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}(f)^{*}
.

Controlling when Beilinson-Flach classes have coefficients in
\wedge^{2}M_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}(f)^{*}
is crucial for us. Indeed, we want to construct classes to compare with higher cyclotomic classes, drawing from the decomposition of Galois representations. Higher cyclotomic classes give values for the
L
-function associated to the representation
\wedge^{2}\rho_{f,v}
, hence if we want to construct classes to compare them with, the only natural choice is the
\wedge^{2}
-component. In the sequel we will rely heavily on the properties of the non-compactified classes in order to draw conclusions, working around the cuspidal contribution.

6.4
Differential forms attached to cusp forms

In this subsection we seek a differential form to pair with the image of the compactified Beilinson-Flach classes under
r_{\mathcal{D}}
(see diagram in figure
1
). Such a differential would belong to the cohomology group
H^{k+1,k+1}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{k},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{k})
. In order to construct it we start from modular forms. Recall from Subsection
4.3
that the middle Hodge component is isomorphic to
(S_{k+2}(N)\otimes\overline{S_{k+2}(N)})\oplus(\overline{S_{k+2}(N)}\otimes S_{k+2}(N))
. Starting from cusp forms, the Eichler-Shimura isomorphism associates:

\displaystyle S_{k+2}(N)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Fil^{1}H^{k+1}_{\dR}(W_{k},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{k})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\omega_{f}

where
\omega_{f}
is the unique
(k+1)
-form whose pull-back to
Y_{1}(N)
(through
\mathcal{E}^{k}
) is

(2\pi i)^{k+1}f(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau w^{(k,0)}=(2\pi i)^{k}f(q)\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{q}w^{(k,0)}\in H^{1}_{\dR}(Y_{1}(N),\TSym^{k}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{E})).

Here we are denoting with
w=\mathrm{d}z
the standard section of
\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{E})
, and
w^{(r,s)}=w^{r}\overline{w}^{s}\in\TSym^{k}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{E})
. This prescription does not only define a de Rham cohomology class, but a true differential form.

Since the Eichler-Shimura isomorphism is Hecke-equivariant, the form
\omega_{f}
is in the
f
-isotypical component of the de Rham cohomology group. The dual of
\omega_{f}
under the Poincaré pairing belongs to the dual of
H^{k+1,0}_{\dR}(W_{k},\mathbb{C})
, which is
H^{0,k+1}_{\dR}(W_{k},\mathbb{C})
, and we denote such form with
\eta_{f^{*}}
. We can actually make this explicit:

\eta_{f^{*}}=\frac{1}{(\omega_{f},\overline{\omega_{f}})}\overline{\omega_{f}}\in H^{0,k+1}_{\dR}(W_{k},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{k})\simeq\overline{S_{k+2}(N)}.

The form
\eta_{f^{*}}
lies in the
f^{*}
-isotypical component, as the Hecke operators act on it with eigenvalues which are conjugates of those of
f
. We also have the relation
(\omega_{f},\overline{\omega_{f}})=(-4\pi)^{k+1}\langle f,f\rangle
.

Suppose now that
f
is a
newform
. By Strong Multiplicity One
\omega_{f}
spans the
f
-isotypical component inside the
(k+1,0)
Hodge component, which is
1
-dimensional. Analogously,
\eta_{f}
spans the
f
-isotypical component inside the
(0,k+1)
Hodge component.

Lemma 6.4.1
.

If
f,g\in S_{k+2}(N)^{new}
are newforms, then the
(f,g)
-isotypical component
M_{\dR}(f\otimes g)\otimes\mathbb{C}\leq H^{2k+2}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{k},\mathbb{C})(\varepsilon_{k})
is
4
