-dimensional with basis

\{\omega_{f}\otimes\omega_{g},\omega_{f}\otimes\eta_{g},\eta_{f}\otimes\omega_{g},\eta_{f}\otimes\eta_{g}\}.

Proof.

This follows from the decompositions (
14
) simply by taking the relevant eigenspace.
∎

Let
\Omega_{f,g}=\omega_{f}\otimes\eta_{g}-\eta_{f}\otimes\omega_{g}\in M_{\dR}(f\otimes g)
. It is clear that
\Omega_{f,g}\neq 0
as it comes from the composition of two linearly independent differential forms, hence it determines a
1
-dimensional subspace filtration:

0\subseteq\langle\Omega_{f,g}\rangle\subseteq M_{\dR}(f\otimes g)\otimes\mathbb{C}.

When
f=g
,
M_{\dR}(f\otimes f)
already enjoys a filtration given by the direct sum decomposition:

M_{\dR}(f\otimes f)\simeq\wedge^{2}M_{\dR}(f)\oplus\Sym^{2}M_{\dR}(f)

induced by the action of the involution swapping the two components in the tensor product. As
M_{\dR}(f)
is
2
-dimensional,
\wedge^{2}M_{\dR}(f\otimes f)
is
1
-dimensional, and since
\Omega_{f,f}
is non-zero and belongs to the anti-symmetric component by construction, the following result follows.

Proposition 6.4.2
.

\Omega_{f,f}\in\wedge^{2}M_{\dR}(f)
and it generates the anti-symmetric component.

This amounts to say that the above filtrations coincide. We note that
\Omega_{f,f}
always lies in the
\wedge^{2}
-component, in accordance with the fact that we want to exploit classes there, see Subsubsection
6.3.3
.

7
Regulator formulæ

In this section we prove complex and
p
-adic regulator formulæ linking compactified Beilinson-Flach classes to special values of
L
-functions.

7.1
Archimedean argument

7.1.1
A complex regulator formula

We explain here the complex regulator formula, following
[
BC16
,
KLZ15
]
.

Recall diagram in figure
2
at page
2
and specialise it to
X=\mathscr{W}_{k}
, which has dimension
2k+2
over
\mathbb{Q}
. As we will compute the pairing using the bottom row, we first analyse carefully the cohomology group appearing there. Even though
\mathscr{W}_{k}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, we regard it as being defined over
K_{f}
, as in
7.1.2
we will need to extend coefficients to
K_{f}
. By the isomorphisms (
11
) we obtain

\displaystyle\Ext^{0}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}(K_{f},K)

\displaystyle\simeq W_{0}K\cap F^{0}(K\otimes\mathbb{C}),

\displaystyle\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}(K_{f},H)

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{(W_{0}H\otimes\mathbb{C})}{W_{0}H+F^{0}(H\otimes\mathbb{C})}.

with
H=H_{B}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(2+2k-j))
,
K=H_{B}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(k+1))
.

Moreover, since
H^{2k+2}_{B}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(k+1))
is concentrated in weight
0
, the first isomorphism becomes

(18)

\begin{split}\Ext^{0}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}&(K_{f},H_{B}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(k+1)))\\
&\simeq H^{2k+2}_{B}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(k+1))\cap\Fil^{0}H^{2k+2}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{k},\mathbb{C}(k+1))\\
&=H^{2k+2}_{B}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(k+1))\cap\Fil^{k+1}H^{2k+2}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{k},\mathbb{C}).\end{split}

Regarding the
\Ext^{1}
, we attach the functorial morphism
\pr_{f,f}
induced by the projection
H^{2k+2}_{B}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(2k+2-j))\twoheadrightarrow M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}^{*}(-j)
, as explained in Subsection
4.2
. In this way we have obtained the following diagram, where the
\Ext
-groups are all in the category
\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}
:

(19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mot}^{3+2k}(\mathscr{W}_{k},2+2k-j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mot}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k},k+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathcal{D}}^{3+2k}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(2+2k-j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathcal{D}}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(k+1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{1}(K_{f},H^{2k+2}_{B}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(2+2k-j)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{0}(K_{f},H_{B}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(k+1)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{1}(K_{f},M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}^{*}(-j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{0}(K_{f},M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}(k+1))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{edge}}
edge
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{f,f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{f,f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

Notice that in the right column we are projecting onto a subobject: this is possible since
H^{2k+2}_{B}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(k+1))
decomposes as a direct sum of isotypical components corresponding to cusp forms (see Subsection
4.3
). We are thus using the projection over a direct summand. The
\Ext^{0}
-group with coefficients in (some twist of)
M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}
is the correct group to land in, as we want to pair elements in the bottom row.

With the same argument as before, we can compute

\begin{split}\Ext^{0}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}&(K_{f},M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}(k+1))\\
&\simeq M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}(k+1)\cap\Fil^{0}M_{\dR}(f\otimes f)_{\mathbb{C}}(k+1)\\
&=M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}(k+1)\cap\Fil^{k+1}M_{\dR}(f\otimes f)_{\mathbb{C}}.\end{split}

Lemma 7.1.1
.

We have
\Omega_{f,f}\in\Ext^{0}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}(K_{f},M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}(k+1))
.

Proof.

Clearly
\Omega_{f,f}
belongs to
\Fil^{k+1}M_{\dR}(f\otimes f)_{\mathbb{C}}
, so it suffices to show it also belongs to
M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}(k+1)
. Notice that by definition
M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}(k+1)=M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}\otimes K_{f}(k+1)
so it suffices to show that
\Omega_{f,f}
belongs to the untwisted group.

We will show in Proposition
7.1.6
that
\Omega_{f,f}
lies in the target of the cycle class map: there exists a class in
\mathrm{CH}^{k+1}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})})\otimes K_{f}
such that its image under
\cl_{\dR}
coincides with
\Omega_{f,f}
. The proof of that proposition only uses the fact that
\Omega_{f,f}
is a de Rham class in the middle Hodge component, so we can use the result here. The image of
\mathrm{CH}^{k+1}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})})
under the cycle class map is

\displaystyle H_{B}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Z})\cap H^{k+1,k+1}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{C}).

In particular, this shows that
\Omega_{f,f}
belongs to

\displaystyle H_{B}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Z})\otimes K_{f}\simeq H^{2k+2}_{B}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}).

Since
\Omega_{f,f}
is already in the
(f,f)
-isotypical component, this shows that it belongs to
M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}
.
∎

Remark
.

By construction
\Omega_{f,f}\in\wedge^{2}M_{\dR}(f)
, therefore the above lemma proves that it is in the subspace
\Ext^{0}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}(K_{f},\wedge^{2}M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}(k+1))
.

To summarise, we have shown that
\Omega_{f,f}
is in the second group in the bottom row of the diagram and, by definition,
\Xi_{\mathcal{D}}^{f,f,j}
is in the first group in the same row. There is a natural pairing between those two groups, induced by the natural one between
M_{B}(f\otimes f)_{K_{f}}
and its dual, with target
\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}(K_{f},K_{f}(1+k-j))\simeq K_{f}(1+k-j)
. By commutativity of the corresponding diagram, pairing
\Xi_{\mathcal{D}}^{f,f,j}
with
\Omega_{f,f}
computes the same value as in Deligne cohomology (which is also the same obtainable by pairing elements in the
\Ext
-groups of Betti cohomology).

The resulting computation expresses the relation between the bottom layer of the Beilinson-Flach Euler system and a special value of the derivative of the Rankin-Selberg
L
-function and is foundational for our paper. By the remark at page
Remark
, pairing (the Deligne realisations of)
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}
or
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}
with differential forms arising from cusp forms yields the same result. Since
\Omega_{f,f}
comes from cusp forms, we can compute the pairing as if we were using the original classes. More precisely, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 7.1.2

(
[
BC16
, Proposition 8.3]
)
.

We have

\langle r_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}),\Omega_{f,f}\rangle=\langle r_{\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}),\Omega_{f,f}\rangle.

Remark
.

The proposition makes sense because
\Omega_{f,f}
is canonically associated to a cohomology class with compact support as noted in Subsection
4.2
. Notice that in the above equality, on the left hand side we have less information on the Beilinson-Flach classes, so we have to make up for this with additional information on
\Omega_{f,f}
—namely, that it corresponds to a compactly supported class.

Proposition 7.1.3
.

We have

\langle r_{\mathcal{D}}(\Xi^{k,k,j}),\Omega_{f,f}\rangle=\frac{1+(-1)^{j}}{2}\langle r_{\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}),\Omega_{f,f}\rangle.

Proof.

By linearity and compatibility of
r_{\mathcal{D}}
with pull-backs:

\displaystyle\langle r_{\mathcal{D}}(\Xi^{k,k,j}),\Omega_{f,f}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle r_{\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}),\Omega_{f,f}\rangle+\frac{(-1)^{k+j}}{2}\langle r_{\mathcal{D}}((\rho^{\prime})^{*}\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}),\Omega_{f,f}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle r_{\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}),\Omega_{f,f}\rangle+\frac{(-1)^{k+j}}{2}\langle r_{\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}),(\rho^{\prime})^{*}\Omega_{f,f}\rangle.

By definition
\Omega_{f,f}
is an antisymmetric tensor with components of degree
k+1
, hence
(\rho^{\prime})^{*}\Omega_{f,f}=(-1)^{k+1}s(\Omega_{f,f})=(-1)^{k}\Omega_{f,f}
. This proves the claim.
∎

We are now in a position to prove the following version of the regulator formula over
X_{1}(N)^{2}
.

Theorem 7.1.4
.

If
j
is even and
0\leq j\leq k
, then the following formula holds.

\begin{split}\langle r_{\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}),\Omega_{f,f}\rangle&=\langle r_{\mathcal{D}}(\Xi^{k,k,j}),\Omega_{f,f}\rangle=\langle\Xi^{f,f,j}_{\mathcal{D}},\Omega_{f,f}\rangle\\
&=(2\pi i)^{2(k-j)}\frac{(-1)^{k-j}}{2(\omega_{f},\overline{\omega_{f}})}\Bigl(\frac{k!}{(k-j)!}\Bigr)^{2}L^{\prime}(f,f,j+1).\end{split}

Proof.

The first equality is given by the last proposition since
j
is even. The second equality comes from the fact that if we extend diagram
2
with the morphisms from (
19
), the resulting diagram is still commutative, because the involved morphisms commute with cup-products and push-forwards.

Proposition
7.1.2
shows that the pairings all coincide with
\langle\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mathcal{D}},\Omega_{f,f}\rangle
. Applying
[
KLZ15
, Theorem 6.2.9]
we deduce the explicit value of the pairing. Indeed, the pull-back of
\Omega_{f,f}
to
Y_{1}(N)^{2}
equals the differential form used there by construction, as we assumed
j
even. The cohomology class they denote
\mathrm{AJ}_{\mathcal{H},f,f}(\mathrm{Eis}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mathcal{H},1,N})
corresponds to the class we denoted
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\mathcal{D}}
. This follows from the fact that if a Hodge structure has non-positive weight, its Deligne and absolute Hodge cohomologies are isomorphic. Hence, the cited theorem computes the pairing which in our notation is

\langle\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\mathcal{D}},\Omega_{f,f}\rangle.

By the same argument that we used for compactified classes, we deduce that this equals
\langle\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mathcal{D}},\Omega_{f,f}\rangle
. Therefore, all the pairings in the statement of the theorem compute to the expression on the right hand side of
[
KLZ15
, Theorem 6.2.9]
, which proves the claim.
∎

Remark
.

More generally, the regulator formula links the Deligne realisation of
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k^{\prime},j}
with
L^{\prime}(f,g,j+1)
through the pairing with
\Omega_{f,g}
. However, we will only need the version with
f=g
, so we restricted to this case for simplicity.

In
[
KLZ15
]
Deligne cohomology is replaced with absolute Hodge cohomology. In the case under consideration the two cohomologies agree, so the above form of the theorem is equivalent to that with absolute Hodge cohomology.

Remark on symmetry eigenspaces

As remarked above,
\Omega_{f,f}
lives in the subspace
\Ext^{0}_{\mathrm{MH}_{K_{f}}}(K_{f},\wedge^{2}M_{B}(f)_{K_{f}}(k+1))
, so it pairs non-trivially only with the
\wedge^{2}
-component of
\widetilde{\Xi}^{f,f,j}_{\mathcal{D}}
, i.e. that in the cohomology of
\wedge^{2}M_{\dR}(f)^{*}
. When
j
is even,
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,j]}_{\mathcal{D}}
is in the antisymmetric component too, but
\widetilde{\Xi}^{f,f,j}_{\mathcal{D}}
has no such property in general. This is the main reason why we defined the symmetrised classes
\Xi^{k,k,j}
. In any case, after Brunault and Chida’s result the complex regulator formula does not detect this.

It is however important to keep in mind that we are ultimately studying classes in the cohomology of
\wedge^{2}M_{\mathcal{T}}(f)
rather than of the full
M_{\mathcal{T}}(f\otimes f)
. For this reason we defined
\Omega_{f,f}
to detect the antisymmetric component. As for Beilinson-Flach classes, we ensured that the non-compact ones belong to the correct eigenspace with the requirement on
j
, and we worked around the cuspidal defect, as explained in
6.3.3
.

7.1.2
The regulator formula in motivic cohomology

We now interpret the complex regulator formula purely in terms of motivic cohomology.

By looking at the diagrams, it is natural to ask if one could find a preimage of
\Omega_{f,f}
in the motivic cohomology group, so that both pieces of the regulator formula would have a source in motivic cohomology. For this purpose we take advantage of the extra row in de Rham cohomology. The isomorphism (
18
) is such that its composition with
r_{\mathcal{D}}
is the de Rham cycle class map, as explained in
[
EV88
, §7]
. This is coherent with the numerology, since
H^{2k+2}_{\mot}(\mathscr{W}_{k},k+1)\simeq\mathrm{CH}^{k+1}(\mathscr{W}_{k})
. Therefore, it suffices to show an element of the motivic cohomology group whose image under the cycle class map is
\Omega_{f,f}
. Summarising, we are searching for a rational equivalence class
[Z_{f}]\in\mathrm{CH}^{k+1}(\mathscr{W}_{k})
of cycles of codimension
k+1
such that

\displaystyle\cl_{\dR}\colon H_{\mot}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k},k+1)

\displaystyle\to H^{k+1,k+1}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{k},\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle[Z_{f}]

\displaystyle\mapsto C\cdot\Omega_{f,f},\quad C\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

In the remainder of this subsubsection we construct the subvariety
Z_{f}
combining together three different correspondences.

Projection onto
f
-eigenspace

Since
f
is a newform, Strong Multiplicity One implies that its eigenspace in
S_{k+2}(N,\psi)
is
1
-dimensional. The projection onto it can be realised by a Hecke operator: let
f=f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{c}
be a basis of
S_{k+2}(N,\psi)
over
K_{f}
consisting of eigenforms. For every
i=1,2,\ldots,c
there exists a prime
l_{i}
such that
a_{l_{i}}(f)\neq a_{l_{i}}(f_{i})
. If
f_{i}\neq f_{j}
are in the same Galois
G_{K_{f}}
-orbit, then we choose
l_{i}=l_{j}
. This is possible because
a_{l_{i}}(f_{j})=a_{l_{i}}(f_{i})^{\sigma}
for some
\sigma\in G_{K_{f}}
, so either both or none of them equals
a_{l_{i}}(f)\in K_{f}
.

Define the operator:

T_{f}=\prod_{i=1}^{c}\frac{T_{l_{i}}-a_{l_{i}}(f_{i})}{a_{l_{i}}(f)-a_{l_{i}}(f_{i})}\in\mathbb{T}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}K_{f}.

It is easy to see that
T_{f}(f_{i})=0
for every
i=1,2,\ldots,c
and
T_{f}(f)=f
. Therefore
T_{f}
is the
f
-isotypical projector on
S_{k+2}(N,\psi)
. We regard
T_{f}
also as a correspondence on
W_{k}
, yielding functorially a morphism in cohomology. Note that
T_{f}
is a combination of correspondences defined over
\mathbb{Q}
with coefficients in
K_{f}
. Indeed, the chosen basis contains all the Galois conjugates of a given eigenform, under the action of
G_{K_{f}}
. As a consequence, the finite linear combination defined above is fixed by
G_{K_{f}}
, because the coefficients are permuted among themselves thanks to our choices. Therefore, it defines an element of the Hecke algebra
\mathbb{T}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}K_{f}
.

Projection onto the middle Künneth component

Consider the morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{W}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}}

Let
\delta_{\infty}\colon\mathrm{CH}^{*}(\mathscr{W}_{k})\to\mathrm{CH}^{*}(\mathscr{W}_{k})
be the correspondence on
\mathscr{W}_{k}
given by the prescription

\delta_{\infty}([Z])=[Z]-i_{1,*}\circ\pi_{1,*}([Z])-i_{2,*}\circ\pi_{2,*}([Z]).

In
[
DRS12
, §2]
it is proved the action of
\delta_{\infty}
on cohomology is to project onto the middle Künneth component in any degree. In particular

\delta_{\infty}^{*}\colon H^{2k+2}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{k},\mathbb{C})\to\bigl(H^{k+1}_{\dR}(W_{k},\mathbb{C})\bigr)^{\otimes 2}.

Atkin-Lehner involution

Denote with
w_{N}
the Atkin-Lehner involution induced by the action of
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
N&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, and with
\lambda_{N}(f)
the pseudo-eigenvalue of
f
. Notice that
w_{N}
is not defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, but only over
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
, so it is actually only a correspondence on
W_{k,\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}
.

Denote also with
\mathrm{gr}
the graph of a correspondence. For a correspondence
c
from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
, its graph is a subset
\mathrm{gr}(c)\subseteq C_{1}\times C_{2}
.

Definition 7.1.5
.

Let
[Z_{f}]\in\mathrm{CH}^{k+1}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})})\otimes K_{f}
be the class of the cycle
\delta_{\infty}(\mathrm{gr}(T_{f^{*}}\circ w_{N}))
.

The composition
T_{f^{*}}\circ w_{N}
is a correspondence on
W_{k}
, so its graph is a subvariety of
\mathscr{W}_{k}
, and
\delta_{\infty}
has a well-defined action on its equivalence class.

Proposition 7.1.6
.

The above class satisfies
\cl_{\dR}(Z_{f})=\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\Omega_{f,f}
.

In the statement we are comparing classes in de Rham cohomology with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}
, so the base change to
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
and the extension of coefficients to
K_{f}
do not play any role. Thanks to the flatness of
\mathbb{C}
, the equality would still hold over smaller fields, even though we will not use this fact.

Proof.

Let
T=T_{f^{*}}\circ w_{N}
for notational simplicity. Denote with
\Delta\subseteq\mathscr{W}_{k}
the diagonal of
\mathscr{W}_{k}
. By definition

\cl_{\dR}(Z_{f})=\rho\iff\int_{Z_{f}}\omega=\int_{\mathscr{W}_{k}}\omega\rho=(\omega,\rho)\quad\forall\omega.

In particular, if we vary
\omega
in a basis, we can compute
\cl_{\dR}(Z_{f})
as an element of
H^{k+1,k+1}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{k},\mathbb{C})^{\vee}
and then express it as a differential form by duality.
Consider a basis of
H^{k+1,k+1}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{k},\mathbb{C})
determined by the direct sum decomposition given by the Künneth isomorphism. Let
\omega\in H^{k+1,k+1}_{\dR}(\mathscr{W}_{k},\mathbb{C})
be a differential form in this basis, then by a standard property of the pull-back

\int_{Z_{f}}\omega=\int_{\mathrm{gr}(T)}\delta_{\infty}^{*}\omega=\begin{cases}\int_{\mathrm{gr}(T)}\omega&\text{if $\omega\in(H^{k+1}_{\dR}(W_{k},\mathbb{C}))^{\otimes 2}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

The last equality holds because
\delta_{\infty}^{*}
projects on the middle Künneth component. It suffices then to consider forms in a basis of
(H^{k+1}_{\dR}(W_{k},\mathbb{C}))^{\otimes 2}
. Consider a basis of
H^{k+1}_{\dR}(W_{k},\mathbb{C})
consisting of differential forms associated to cusp forms, which exists by the isomorphism (
13
).
Write
\omega=\omega_{1}\otimes\omega_{2}
with
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
in this basis. Again by properties of the pull-back we have

\int_{Z_{f}}\omega=\int_{\mathrm{gr}(T)}\delta_{\infty}^{*}\omega=\int_{\mathrm{gr}(T)}\omega=\int_{\Delta}\omega_{1}\otimes w_{N}^{*}T_{f^{*}}^{*}\omega_{2}.

By construction
T_{f^{*}}
projects onto
M_{\dR}(f^{*})\otimes\mathbb{C}
, which is generated by
\omega_{f^{*}}
and
\eta_{f^{*}}
. In the first case we are left with

=\int_{\Delta}\omega_{1}\otimes w_{N}^{*}\omega_{f^{*}}=\int_{\Delta}\omega_{1}\otimes\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\omega_{f}=\lambda_{N}(f^{*})(\omega_{1},\omega_{f}).

Since we are working with basis vectors, the cup product
(\omega_{1},\omega_{f})
is non-zero only when
\omega_{1}
is a scalar multiple of the dual of
\omega_{f}
, and equals
1
exactly when
\omega_{1}=-(\omega_{f})^{*}=-\eta_{f^{*}}
. The argument then shows that

\displaystyle\cl_{\dR}(Z_{f})(-\lambda_{N}(f^{*})^{-1}\eta_{f^{*}}\otimes\omega_{f^{*}})=1

similarly

\displaystyle\cl_{\dR}(Z_{f})(\lambda_{N}(f^{*})^{-1}\omega_{f^{*}}\otimes\eta_{f^{*}})=1.

Therefore
\cl_{\dR}
coincides with
(-\lambda_{N}(f^{*})^{-1}\eta_{f^{*}}\otimes\omega_{f^{*}})^{*}+(\lambda_{N}(f^{*})^{-1}\omega_{f^{*}}\otimes\eta_{f^{*}})^{*}
. To compute these observe:

(\omega_{f^{*}}\otimes\eta_{f^{*}},\eta_{f}\otimes\omega_{f})=(\omega_{f^{*}},\eta_{f})\cdot(\eta_{f^{*}},\omega_{f})=-1.

So we get as a result
\lambda_{N}(f^{*})(\omega_{f}\otimes\eta_{f}-\eta_{f}\otimes\omega_{f})
. This proves the proposition.
∎

In order to use the correspondences
T_{f^{*}}
and
w_{N}
, we base changed to
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
and extended coefficients to
K_{f}
. We then need to modify our original diagram to reflect these changes. Notably, we now start from the motivic cohomology of
\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}
. However, the two bottom rows of diagram (
19
) are unchanged. Indeed,
\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}
is still defined over
\mathbb{Q}
(hence over
K_{f}
) and in Betti and de Rham cohomology we deduce by the Künneth isomorphism:

\displaystyle H_{B}^{i}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})},K_{f}(n))

\displaystyle\simeq H_{B}^{i}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(n))\otimes H_{B}^{0}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),K_{f})=H_{B}^{i}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(n)),

\displaystyle H_{\dR}^{i}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})},K_{f}(n))

\displaystyle\simeq H_{\dR}^{i}(\mathscr{W}_{k},K_{f}(n)).

Thanks to this remark we can use the commutativity of the diagram in figure
2
to finish the proof of the the next theorem.

Definition 7.1.7
.

We define a cohomology class in
H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),1+k-j)\otimes K_{f}
as
b_{f}=\langle\Xi^{k,k,j},Z_{f}\rangle
.

Theorem 7.1.8

(Complex regulator formula)
.

If
j
is even and
0\leq j\leq k
, then
b_{f}
satisfies:

(20)

\begin{split}r_{\mathcal{D}}(b_{f})&=\langle r_{\mathcal{D}}(\Xi^{k,k,j}),\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\Omega_{f,f}\rangle\\
&=(2\pi i)^{2(k-j)}\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\frac{(-1)^{k-j}}{2(\omega_{f},\overline{\omega_{f}})}\Bigl(\frac{k!}{(k-j)!}\Bigr)^{2}L^{\prime}(f,f,j+1).\end{split}

Proof.

This is a direct application of the complex regulator formula proved above. The first equality is implied by the commutativity of the diagram in figure
2
. The second equality is Theorem
7.1.4
, which applies because
j
is even.
∎

Remark
.

b_{f}
is a motivic source for
L
-values, as it lies in the motivic cohomology group
H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),1+k-j)\otimes K_{f}
and its image under
r_{\mathcal{D}}
yields
complex

L
-values. In the sequel we will also relate
b_{f}
to
p
-adic

L
-values, by computing its image under
r_{\et}
.

7.2
Non-archimedean argument

In this subsection we apply the machinery of Subsection
3.2
to the case under consideration. Recall that at the end of that subsection we constructed a commutative diagram (figure
4
) by studying edge maps in the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence. When we specialise to
X=\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}
(then
d=k+1
) the bottom line of the diagram reads:

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),H^{2k+2}_{\et}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2+2k-j)))\times H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),H^{2k+2}_{\et}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1+k)))_{K_{f}}\\
\to H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1+k-j))\otimes K_{f}.

We had to extend coefficients of the second group to
K_{f}
after the definition of
Z_{f}
. Inside the cohomology groups
H^{2k+2}_{\et}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\star))
we can find the
p
-adic Galois representations associated to the pair
(f,f)
, which are the isotypical component in étale cohomology
M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)
and its dual
M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}
. Therefore, by inserting projections, we obtain an expression purely in terms of the cohomology of
M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)
:

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-j))\times H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)(1+k))\otimes K_{f}\\
\to H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1+k-j))\otimes K_{f}.

The above row is connected to the previous one compatibly by projection maps. We can then equivalently compute the pairing in the cohomology of
M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)
.
Notice also that since
f
is a cusp form, its eigenspace is concentrated in the middle cohomological degree. This is equivalent to the fact that the representation
\rho_{f,v}
is pure of weight
k+1
.

Furthermore, by replacing the étale regulator with the syntomic regulator we can apply the same argument to construct a commutative diagram in syntomic cohomology. Recall that under suitable hypotheses—in particular over
p
-adic fields—the first étale and syntomic cohomology groups are related by the Bloch-Kato exponential.

We introduce the following notation:

Z_{f}^{\diamond}=r_{\diamond}(Z_{f}),\quad\diamond\in\{\et,\syn\}.

Suppose we are able to compute
\langle r_{\syn}(\Xi^{k,k,j}),Z_{f}^{\syn}\rangle
. The resulting element belongs to
H^{1}_{\syn}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1+k-j))\otimes K_{f}
. There is then a map:

H^{1}_{\syn}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1+k-j))\to H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1+k-j))

given by the Bloch-Kato exponential, as
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1+k-j)
is crystalline hence de Rham. Notice that we moved to étale cohomology over local fields.

We would like to use this map to link the syntomic and étale diagrams, but there is an obstacle given by the fact that over
p
-adic fields the edge map from
H^{3+2k}_{\et}
to
E_{2}^{0,3+2k}
is not necessarily zero, as explained in Subsection
3.2
. This problem would defy the construction of our diagram, because we could not map Beilinson-Flach classes to
E_{2}^{1,2k+2}
. However, the kernel of the edge map to
E_{2}^{0,3+2k}
contains all the cohomology classes that are localisations of global classes. Our construction is chiefly global, which is to say that we are dealing with classes in the cohomology of
\mathscr{W}_{k}
over a number field; and the classes that we consider in the local diagram are their localisations. Therefore, they lie in the kernel of the edge map to
E_{2}^{0,3+2k}
, and thus they can safely be mapped to
E_{2}^{1,2k+2}
.

By this discussion, the following diagram commutes as we are feeding it with global classes (we omit the coefficients for better readability):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\syn}^{2k+3}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})})\times H_{\syn}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\syn}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mot}^{2k+3}(\mathscr{W}_{k})\times H_{\mot}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\et}^{2k+3}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})})\times H_{\et}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\!\otimes\!f)^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\times H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\!\otimes\!f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\syn}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{loc}\,\circ\,r_{\et}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{f,f}\circ\mathrm{edge}}

Therefore, simply by applying
\exp
we would obtain the value of the pairing in
local
étale cohomology. It suffices then to compute the pairing in syntomic cohomology.

The rest of the argument is organised as follows:

•

we will show that
Z_{f}^{\syn}
is canonically associated to a compactly supported cohomology class;

•

we will split the pairing in syntomic cohomology in the sum of three terms;

•

the first term computes the pairing on the open variety
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
, we will link it to the
p
-adic value;

•

the other terms compute the “cuspidal contribution”, we will show it is always zero.

Remark
.

The material of this section applies also to the ordinary case, with some changes. Firstly, Lemma
7.2.1
works only for the Satake parameter which is also a
p
-adic unit. By contrast, in the supersingular case it works with both choices. Secondly, in Theorem
7.2.6
and the subsequent ones, the geometric
p
-adic
L
-function must be replaced with Hida’s
3
-variable
p
-adic
L
-function. This section gives then also an alternative proof of the
p
-adic regulator formula in the ordinary case. The main difference with the proof contained in
[
Das16
, §6.3]
is that that proof only works in weight
2
as stated, while the proof presented here works in higher weights too. Moreover, Dasgupta’s proof yields a formula for points
(0,0,1+\vartheta)
in our normalisation, with
\vartheta
a
p
-power conductor Dirichlet character, while the one presented here covers points
(k_{0},k_{0},j_{0}+1)
with
0\leq j_{0}\leq k_{0}
.

Study of
Z_{f}^{\syn}

We recall that syntomic cohomology is characterised by sitting in a long exact sequence (induced by the Leray spectral sequence):

\cdots\to H_{\syn}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(k+1))\\
\to F^{0}H_{\dR}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(k+1))=F^{k+1}H_{\dR}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Q}_{p})\\
\to H_{\rig}^{2k+2}(\mathscr{W}_{k,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(k+1))\to\cdots

We can thus write
Z_{f}^{\syn}=(\xi,\lambda)
with
\xi
and
\lambda
respectively rigid and de Rham cohomology classes. By the compatibility of the regulator
r_{\syn}
and
\cl_{\dR}
in the Leray spectral sequence
[
Bes00
, Theorem 7.5]
, the composition of
r_{\syn}
and the morphism to de Rham cohomology in the long exact sequence coincides with
\cl_{\dR}
. Therefore
\lambda
is equal to the image of
\cl_{\dR}(Z_{f})=\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\Omega_{f,f}
under the extension of the coefficients to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Our first aim is to compute it.

We start by studying the behaviour of
\omega_{f}
and
\eta_{f}
in syntomic cohomology. In order to pass to de Rham cohomology with
(K_{f}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-coefficients we must start from classes defined over
K_{f}
rather than over
\mathbb{C}
. According to
[
KLZ17
, §6.1]
the differentials
\omega_{f},\eta_{f}
can be easily modified to be
K_{f}
-rational. Explicitly, if we define

\omega_{f}^{\prime}=\tau(\psi^{-1})\omega_{f},\quad\eta_{f}^{\prime}=\tau(\psi^{-1})\eta_{f}

then
\omega_{f}^{\prime},\eta_{f}^{\prime}\in M_{\dR}(f)
, i.e. they are
K_{f}
-rational. We can then safely regard
\omega_{f}^{\prime},\eta_{f}^{\prime}\in M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

Lemma 7.2.1
.

Let
\alpha_{f}
be the Satake parameter of
f
of smallest valuation. Then the eigenspace
V_{\varphi}(\alpha_{f})
in
M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is a complement of
\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. If
f
is supersingular, then
V_{\varphi}(\beta_{f})
is also a complement of
\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, and
\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is not a
\varphi
-eigenspace.

Proof.

By transport of structure via the isomorphism
C_{\mathrm{crys}}
, the module
M_{\rig}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\simeq M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\simeq D_{\mathrm{crys}}(M_{\etbar}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
enjoys a semi-linear action of Frobenius
\varphi
(by comparison with rigid cohomology) and an action of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
(by comparison with étale cohomology). It also has a natural Hodge filtration. Being in the image of the functor
D_{\mathrm{crys}}
, it is a
weakly admissible
filtered
\varphi
-module in the sense of
[
BC09
, Definition 8.2.1]
, that is its Newton polygon lies on or above its Hodge polygon, equivalently the rightmost endpoint of the Newton polygon lies on or above the rightmost endpoint of the Hodge polygon. Since the category of weakly admissible filtered
\varphi
-modules is abelian, this property is preserved by taking subobjects and subquotients.

The Hodge filtration of
\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
has a unique non-zero piece in degree
k+1
, which is
1
-dimensional. Therefore the rightmost endpoint of the Hodge polygon is
(1,k+1)
. Suppose now that
\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is also a
\varphi
-eigenspace. Since
\varphi
acts on
M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
with eigenvalues
\alpha_{f}
and
\beta_{f}
,
\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
must be equal to either eigenspace. Suppose it equals
V_{\varphi}(\alpha_{f})
, then the slope is
v_{p}(\alpha_{f})
, so the rightmost endpoint of the Newton polygon is
(1,v_{p}(\alpha_{f}))
. However
\alpha_{f}
is the Satake parameter of smallest valuation, so we have
v_{p}(\alpha_{f})<k+1
. This contradicts the fact that
\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is a weakly admissible module. Therefore we deduce that
\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is
not
the eigenspace
V_{\varphi}(\alpha_{f})
. Finally, the two
1
-dimensional subspaces
V_{\varphi}(\alpha_{f})
and
\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
of
M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
do not coincide, so they must be complementary, given that the total space is
2
-dimensional.

If in addition we suppose that
f
is supersingular, we have also
0<v_{p}(\alpha_{f})<k+1
, and the same inequalities hold for
\beta_{f}
. Therefore, the argument shows that
\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
cannot coincide with
V_{\varphi}(\beta_{f})
as well, so in this case it cannot be a
\varphi
-eigenspace at all.
∎

Remark
.

In the ordinary case
v_{p}(\alpha_{f})=0
and
v_{p}(\beta_{f})=k+1
, so
\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
might coincide with the eigenspace
V_{\varphi}(\beta_{f})
.

Since
\eta_{f}^{\prime}
is a generator of
M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}/\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, by the above lemma it has a
unique
lift to the eigenspace where
\varphi
acts as
\alpha_{f}
. Clearly there are several choices of a lift to
M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, with an ambiguity given by an element of
\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. Thanks to the next lemma this ambiguity does not play any role in our future computations.

Lemma 7.2.2
.

Let
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
be two lifts of
\eta_{f}^{\prime}
. Then
\omega_{f}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{1}-\eta_{1}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime}=\omega_{f}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{2}-\eta_{2}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The difference of the two is:

(\omega_{f}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{1}-\eta_{1}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime})-(\omega_{f}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{2}-\eta_{2}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime})=\omega_{f}^{\prime}\otimes(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})-(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime}.

Now
\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}\in\Fil^{1}M_{\dR}(f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
which is a
1
-dimensional
K_{f}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-vector space. By writing
\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}=u\omega_{f}^{\prime}
for some
u\in K_{f}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
the claim follows.
∎

Since we will only consider linear combinations of this kind, we can choose as a “favourite” lift the unique lift
\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\in V_{\varphi}(\alpha_{f})
satisfying
\varphi(\eta^{\alpha_{f}})=\alpha_{f}\eta^{\alpha_{f}}
.

The above argument proves that the image of
\tau(\psi^{-1})^{2}\Omega_{f,f}
under extension of scalars to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
can be identified with
\omega_{f}^{\prime}\otimes\eta^{\alpha_{f}}-\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime}
. We sum up this discussion in the following proposition.

Proposition 7.2.3
.

Z_{f}^{\syn}
is represented by the pair
(\xi,\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\tau(\psi^{-1})^{-2}(\omega_{f}^{\prime}\otimes\eta^{\alpha_{f}}-\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime}))
.

We now study the situation over the non-proper
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
. The analogue of the long exact sequence above holds for cohomology with compact support:

\cdots\to H_{\syn,c}^{2k+2}((\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(k+1))\\
\to F^{0}H_{\dR,c}^{2k+2}((\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(k+1))\\
\to H_{\rig,c}^{2k+2}((\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(k+1))\to\cdots

Recall that
\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\tau(\psi^{-1})^{2}\Omega_{f,f}
belongs to
M_{\dR}(f\otimes f)
, which has canonical inclusions as a direct summand of the cohomology of
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
with and without compact support. Therefore
\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\tau(\psi^{-1})^{2}\Omega_{f,f}
has a canonical image in compactly supported de Rham cohomology, under extension of scalars to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. By the above exact sequence, the preimage of a compactly supported de Rham cohomology class is a compactly supported syntomic cohomology class. By putting everything together we deduce the following result.

Proposition 7.2.4
.

There exists a canonical compactly supported cohomology class
Z^{\syn}_{f,c}\in H^{2k+2}_{\syn,c}((\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(k+1))
that maps to
Z_{f}^{\syn}
in
H^{2k+2}_{\syn}((\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(k+1))
.

Splitting the pairing

The pairing that we are interested in computing is:

\langle r_{\syn}(\Xi^{k,k,j}),Z_{f}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn,\mathscr{W}_{k}}.

Since our aim is to split this computation into an “open” and a “cuspidal” part, the standard strategy would be to use the Gysin long exact sequence induced by the embedding
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{W}_{k}
. In our case it suffices to use the existence of Gysin maps in the category, and the following projection formula due to Besser. The theorem is stated in terms of finite-polynomial cohomology, of which syntomic cohomology is a particular case.

Theorem 7.2.5

(
[
Bes12
, Corollary 5.3]
)
.

Let
\iota\colon X\to Y
be a morphism of smooth integral
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-schemes, and identify the groups
H^{2\dim(X)+1}_{\fp,c}(X)\simeq K
and
H^{2\dim(Y)+1}_{\fp,c}(Y)\simeq K
via the respective trace maps. Let
d=\dim(Y)-\dim(X)
. Let
x\in H^{j}_{\fp,c}(X,m)
and
y\in H^{i}_{\fp}(Y,n)
, and suppose
i+j=2\dim(X)+1
and
n+m>\dim(X)
. Then there exists a push-forward map in any degree

\displaystyle\iota_{*}\colon H^{\bullet}_{\fp}(X,d_{0})\to H^{\bullet+2d}_{\fp}(Y,d_{0}+d)

for all
d_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, and

\displaystyle y\cup\iota_{*}x=\iota^{*}y\cup x.

In our case,
\mathscr{W}_{k}
and
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
are both schemes with a model over
\mathbb{Z}[1/N]
, so they can be regarded as smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-schemes; and their base changes to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})
can be regarded as smooth
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})}
-schemes. The embedding
\iota\colon\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{W}_{k}
is a smooth morphism, so we are in a position to apply the theorem with
d=0
. Moreover,
Z_{f}^{\syn}\in H^{2k+2}_{\syn}((\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(k+1))
is associated to a canonical compactly supported class
Z^{\syn}_{f,c}
by Proposition
7.2.4
. If we choose

x=Z^{\syn}_{f,c},\quad y=r_{\syn}(\Xi^{k,k,j})

the hypotheses of the theorem are verified, since the sums of degrees and twists are

\displaystyle 2+2k+3+2k=5+4k=2\dim(\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k})+1,

\displaystyle k+1+2+2k-j=3+3k-j>2\dim(\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}).

We can thus apply the theorem to find

\mathrm{tr}_{\mathscr{W}_{k}}(r_{\syn}(\Xi^{k,k,j})\cup\iota_{*}Z_{f,c}^{\syn})=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}}(\iota^{*}r_{\syn}(\Xi^{k,k,j})\cup Z_{f,c}^{\syn}).

We can now make the following consideration:

1.

\iota_{*}Z_{f,c}^{\syn}=Z_{f}^{\syn}
since they are represented by the same pair of cohomology classes (in rigid and de Rham cohomology,
M_{\star}(f\otimes f)
has canonical isomorphisms between cohomologies of
\mathscr{W}_{k}
and
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
with and without compact support);

2.

\iota^{*}r_{\syn}(\Xi^{k,k,j})=r_{\syn}(\iota^{*}\Xi^{k,k,j})
by compatibility of regulators and pull-backs, and by construction of
\Xi^{k,k,j}

\iota^{*}\Xi^{k,k,j}=\frac{1}{2}\iota^{*}\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}+\frac{(-1)^{k+j}}{2}\iota^{*}(\rho^{\prime})^{*}\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}.

Now
\iota^{*}(\rho^{\prime})^{*}=(\rho^{\prime}\circ\iota)^{*}=(\iota\circ\rho^{\prime})^{*}
because the two morphisms commute. Indeed,
\rho^{\prime}
swaps the two components of the fibre product, while
\iota
embeds
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
into
\mathscr{W}_{k}
. The two compositions become then, for every
P,Q\in\mathcal{E}^{k}
:

\iota\circ\rho^{\prime}(P,Q)=\iota(Q,P)=(Q,P)=\rho^{\prime}(P,Q)=\rho^{\prime}\circ\iota(P,Q).

We deduce

\iota^{*}\Xi^{k,k,j}=\frac{1}{2}\iota^{*}\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}+\frac{(-1)^{k+j}}{2}(\rho^{\prime})^{*}\iota^{*}\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}.

The inner pull-back unravels as:

(21)

\begin{split}\iota^{*}\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}&=\iota^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{BF}}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}+i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))\\
&=\iota^{*}(\widetilde{\mathrm{BF}}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot})+\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))=\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}+\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta})).\end{split}

Finally, putting everything together:

(22)

\begin{split}\iota^{*}\Xi^{k,k,j}&=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}+\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))\bigr)+\frac{(-1)^{k+j}}{2}\bigl((\rho^{\prime})^{*}\mathrm{BF}^{k,k,j}_{\mot}+(\rho^{\prime})^{*}\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))\bigr)\\
&=\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\mot}+\frac{1}{2}\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))+\frac{(-1)^{k+j}}{2}(\rho^{\prime})^{*}\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta})).\end{split}

By linearity of the trace map, we conclude that

(23)

\begin{split}\langle r_{\syn}(\Xi^{k,k,j}),Z_{f}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn,\mathscr{W}_{k}}&=\langle\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn},Z_{f,c}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn,\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}}\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\langle\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))_{\syn},Z_{f,c}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn,\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}}\\
&+\frac{(-1)^{k+j}}{2}\langle\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))_{\syn},(\rho^{\prime})^{*}Z_{f,c}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn,\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}}.\end{split}

We have therefore written the sought pairing as the sum of three terms. The first is nothing other than the pairing of the non-compactified Beilinson-Flach classes with
Z_{f,c}^{\syn}
on the open variety, while the other two represent the contributions at the cuspidal locus. In the remainder of this subsection we will evaluate them separately, showing that the first contribution gives a
p
-adic
L
-value, while the others vanish.

By comparing equations (
21
) and (
22
) we also notice that the difference between
\iota^{*}\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}
and
\iota^{*}\Xi^{k,k,j}
is supported only on the cuspidal locus. Therefore, the pairings of their syntomic realisations with
Z_{f}^{\syn}
differ only by some contributions of the form
\langle\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))_{\syn},-\rangle_{\syn}
.

Remark
.

The argument essentially requires the smoothness of
\mathscr{W}_{k}
, it is then clear that using
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{k}\times\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{k}
would not have been possible. Since we also need properness to apply Hodge theory, we conclude that enlarging the variety we work on to
\mathscr{W}_{k}
is truly essential. No other variety “in between” is both smooth and proper.

Computing the first term

We now compute the first pairing in equation (
23
). Reasoning as we did for étale cohomology, we can use the edge map and the projection onto the
(f,f)
-isotypical component to link compatibly the row giving the pairing in syntomic cohomology

H^{3+2k}_{\syn}((\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(2+2k-j))\times H^{2+2k}_{\syn,c}((\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(k+1))\\
\xrightarrow{\langle,\rangle_{\syn}}H^{1}_{\syn}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1+k-j))

with the following row

H^{1}(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},M_{\rig}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-j))\times H^{0}(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N})},M_{\rig,c}(f\otimes f)(k+1))\\
\xrightarrow{\langle,\rangle_{f,f}}H^{1}_{\syn}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1+k-j)).

The two rows together form a commutative diagram. We can therefore compute the pairing along the latter one. To go even further, one can compose every map from the first row to the second, with an isomorphism, to obtain as the composition the syntomic Abel-Jacobi map. Since these isomorphisms are compatible with the cup-product and the trace map, all three rows compute the same pairing, in particular

\langle\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn},Z_{f,c}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn}=\langle\mathrm{AJ}_{\syn}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn}),\mathrm{AJ}_{\syn}(Z_{f,c}^{\syn})\rangle_{f,f}.

By
[
KLZ15
, Proposition 6.3.1]
the pairing
\langle\mathrm{AJ}_{\syn}(-),\tilde{\lambda}\rangle_{f,f}
depends only on the class of
\tilde{\lambda}
in de Rham cohomology. This result comes from the fact that the Abel-Jacobi map has as target a quotient of
M_{\dR}(f\otimes f)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. We can then compute the above pairing regardless of the associated rigid cohomology class. By Propositions
7.2.3
and
7.2.4
and since
M_{\dR}(f\otimes f)
has canonical isomorphisms between cohomologies of
\mathscr{W}_{k}
and
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
with compact support, the image of
Z_{f,c}^{\syn}
in de Rham cohomology is the class of
\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\tau(\psi^{-1})^{-2}(\omega_{f}^{\prime}\otimes\eta^{\alpha_{f}}-\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime})
. Therefore the pairing computes to

\langle\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn},Z_{f,c}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn}=\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\tau(\psi^{-1})^{-2}\langle\mathrm{AJ}_{\syn}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn}),\omega_{f}^{\prime}\otimes\eta^{\alpha_{f}}-\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime})\rangle_{f,f}.

The following theorem links explicitly the pairing in syntomic cohomology with the Rankin-Selberg
p
-adic
L
-function.

Theorem 7.2.6

(
[
KLZ15
, Theorem 6.5.9 and Remark 6.5.10]
)
.

Let
\alpha_{f}
be the Satake parameter of
f
of smallest valuation. If
E(f,f,j+1)\neq 0
, then

\langle\mathrm{AJ}_{\syn}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn}),\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime}\rangle\\
=(-1)^{k-j+1}k!\binom{k}{j}\tau(\psi^{-1})^{2}\frac{E(f)E^{*}(f)}{E(f,f,j+1)}L_{p}^{\mathrm{geom}}(F,F)(k,k,j+1).

Remark
.

Theorem 6.5.9 in
[
KLZ15
]
states the above result for the case of
f
ordinary. Remark 6.5.10 therein covers the case of
f
supersingular, by using the geometric
p
-adic
L
-function.

Lemma 7.2.7
.

We have

\langle\mathrm{AJ}_{\syn}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn,N}),\omega_{f}^{\prime}\otimes\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\rangle=(-1)^{j+1}\langle\mathrm{AJ}_{\syn}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn,N}),\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime}\rangle.

Proof.

The proof boils down to considering the action of the involution swapping the two Künneth components in cohomology and its relation with the involution interchanging the two components of the fibre product.

The non-compactified Beilinson-Flach classes live in the cohomology of
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
and of
Y_{1}(N)^{2}
. We regard them and the involved differentials as classes in the cohomology of
Y_{1}(N)^{2}
. The differential forms are then in the second cohomology group, for which the Künneth isomorphism reads

H^{2}(Y_{1}(N)^{2},\TSym^{[k,k]}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(2))\simeq\bigl(H^{1}(Y_{1}(N),\TSym^{k}\mathscr{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{E})(1))\bigr)^{\otimes 2}.

Let
s
be the map that swaps the factors in the tensor product, and
\rho
the morphism exchanging the two factors of
Y_{1}(N)^{2}
. Since the isomorphism is induced by the cup product,
s
and
\rho^{*}
differ by a sign that depends on the degree. We deduce
\omega_{f}^{\prime}\otimes\eta^{\alpha_{f}}=s(\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime})=-\rho^{*}(\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime})
. Now
[
KLZ17
, Proposition 5.2.3]
implies

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{AJ}_{\syn}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn,N}),\omega_{f}^{\prime}\otimes\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{AJ}_{\syn}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn,N}),-\rho^{*}(\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\rho^{*}(\mathrm{AJ}_{\syn}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn,N})),\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j+1}\langle\mathrm{AJ}_{\syn}(\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn,N}),\eta^{\alpha_{f}}\otimes\omega_{f}^{\prime}\rangle.

∎

Remark
.

The argument of the proof would apply to the second version of the compactified classes, though not to the first one, because of the extra correction term
i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta})
. Indeed, this term is defined asymmetrically with respect to the two factors of the fibre product. There is then little hope that
\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}
is in any of the eigenspaces for the swapping involution in general (see Subsubsection
6.3.3
), while the classes
\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}
and
\Xi^{k,k,j}
show a better behaviour.

The above combine into

Theorem 7.2.8
.

If
j
is even,
0\leq j\leq k
and
E(f,f,j+1)\neq 0
, then

\langle\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn},Z_{f,c}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn}=(-1)^{k}k!\binom{k}{j}\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\frac{2E(f)E^{*}(f)}{E(f,f,j+1)}L_{p}^{\mathrm{geom}}(F,F)(k,k,j+1).

Computing the cuspidal terms

It remains to compute the other terms on the right hand side of equation (
23
). As we have shown that the first one computes to a
p
-adic
L
-value, the second and third one have to be regarded as “error terms”. We now show that they vanish, by showing that the cohomology class corresponding to
\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))
is already zero in motivic cohomology.

Lemma 7.2.9
.

We have
\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))=0
.

The proof of this lemma is contained in the proof of
[
BC16
, Proposition 9.4]
. We report here a sketch for the reader’s benefit.

Sketch of the proof.

The core idea is that the defect
i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta})
is the push forward of a cohomology class of the cuspidal locus, so pulling it back to the
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
vanishes by an excision argument.

By Theorem
4.1.3
we can equivalently consider
i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta})
in the cohomology of
U^{k,k}
. We have to show that we have
(\eta_{1})^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))=0
, where
\eta_{1}
is the open embedding
\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}\to U^{k,k}
. Let
i_{1}\colon Z^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}\to\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,*}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
be the canonical closed embedding and
j_{1}\colon\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}\to\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,*}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}
the complementary open embedding. Then
\eta_{1}
factors as
j_{2}\circ j_{1}
in the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,*}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{k,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathrm{cusp}}}

By commutativity
j_{2}^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))=(i_{1})_{*}(\xi_{\beta})
. Considering the long exact localisation sequence induced by
Z^{k}\to\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{k,*}\to\mathcal{E}^{k}
, we deduce
j_{1}^{*}\circ(i_{1})_{*}=0
.
∎

The lemma clearly implies that
\langle\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))_{\syn},\bullet\rangle=0
for all choices of the second class.

Conclusion

The above results taken together prove the following.

Theorem 7.2.10
.

If
j
is even,
0\leq j\leq k
and
E(f,f,j+1)\neq 0
, then

\langle r_{\syn}(\Xi^{k,k,j}),Z_{f}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn,\mathscr{W}_{k}}=\langle r_{\syn}(\widetilde{\Xi}^{k,k,j}),Z_{f}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn,\mathscr{W}_{k}}\\
=(-1)^{k}k!\binom{k}{j}\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\frac{2E(f)E^{*}(f)}{E(f,f,j+1)}L_{p}^{\mathrm{geom}}(F,F)(k,k,j+1).

Proof.

By Lemma
7.2.9
, all the terms in equation (
23
) vanish bar the first, hence

\langle r_{\syn}(\Xi^{k,k,j}),Z_{f}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn,\mathscr{W}_{k}}=\langle\mathrm{BF}^{[k,k,j]}_{\syn},Z_{f,c}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn,\mathcal{E}^{k}\times\mathcal{E}^{k}}.

Applying Theorem
7.2.8
shows that this computes to the
p
-adic
L
-value in the statement of the theorem. The other equality follows from the discussion after equation (
23
). Indeed, the difference of the two pairings equals

\frac{(-1)^{k+j}}{2}\langle\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))_{\syn},(\rho^{\prime})^{*}Z_{f,c}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn,\mathcal{E}^{k,k}}-\frac{1}{2}\langle\iota^{*}(i_{\mathrm{cusp},*}(\xi_{\beta}))_{\syn},Z_{f,c}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn,\mathcal{E}^{k,k}}

which is again zero by the proved results.
∎

We then obtain the main theorem of this subsection.

Theorem 7.2.11

(
p
-adic regulator formula)
.

If
j
is even,
0\leq j\leq k
and
E(f,f,j+1)\neq 0
, then

(24)

r_{\syn}(b_{f})=(-1)^{k}k!\binom{k}{j}\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\frac{2E(f)E^{*}(f)}{E(f,f,j+1)}L_{p}^{\mathrm{geom}}(F,F)(k,k,j+1).

Proof.

By the commutativity of the diagram expressing the compatibility of
r_{\syn}
with cup product and trace map, we directly compute

r_{\syn}(b_{f})=r_{\syn}(\langle\Xi^{k,k,j},Z_{f}\rangle)=\langle r_{\syn}(\Xi^{k,k,j}),Z_{f}^{\syn}\rangle_{\syn,\mathscr{W}_{k}}

and we conclude by the previous proposition thanks to the hypothesis on
j
.
∎

Theorem
7.2.11
expresses the syntomic regulator of
b_{f}
in terms of
p
-adic
L
-values. Since étale cohomology is where we truly find
p
-adic Galois representations and their Galois cohomology groups (for example Selmer groups), we also give the following equivalent formulation in terms of the étale regulator of a motivic cohomology class.

Corollary 7.2.12
.

If
j
is even,
0\leq j\leq k
and
E(f,f,j+1)\neq 0
, then the local étale cohomology class
\mathrm{loc}_{p}(r_{\et}(b_{f}))\in H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1+k-j))\otimes K_{f}
satisfies the formula:

(25)

\log(\mathrm{loc}_{p}(r_{\et}(b_{f})))=(-1)^{k}k!\binom{k}{j}\lambda_{N}(f^{*})\frac{2E(f)E^{*}(f)}{E(f,f,j+1)}L_{p}^{\mathrm{geom}}(F,F)(k,k,j+1).

These results are the
p
-adic analogues of Theorem
7.1.8
. Notice also that the parity conditions coincide. We have hence shown that the motivic class
b_{f}
contains both complex and
p
-adic information about special values of
L
-functions.

Remark
.

The
p
-adic
L
-function appearing in equations (
24
) and (
25
) is the geometric
p
-adic
L
-function defined in Theorem
5.2.3
. It turns out that the
L
-function itself—and not just its values—is related to the Beilinson-Flach Euler system. The link is given by the Perrin-Riou regulator, and detailed in the explicit reciprocity law
[
LZ16
, Theorem 7.1.5]
.

8
Beilinson-Flach
K
-elements

Definition 8.0.1
.

The
Beilinson-Flach

K
-element associated to
f
is the element

b_{f}=\langle\Xi^{k,k,j},Z_{f}\rangle\in H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}(1+k-j))\otimes K_{f}.

By definition motivic cohomology groups are a torsion-free graded part of
K
-groups

H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),1+k-j)=\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{gr}_{1+k-j}^{\gamma}K_{2k-2j+1}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}))

which shows that
b_{f}
lives in a finite dimensional vector space. We want to show that it actually lives in a
1
-dimensional vector space of the kind (
15
), and we do so by exploiting the Galois action.

Proposition 8.0.2
.

The Galois group
\mathrm{Gal}({\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}/{\mathbb{Q}})
acts on
b_{f}
through the character
\psi^{-1}
, i.e.
b_{f}^{\sigma}=(\psi)_{Gal}^{-1}(\sigma)b_{f}
. Explicitly, the automorphism
\sigma_{q}\colon\zeta\mapsto\zeta^{q}
acts as
b_{f}^{\sigma_{q}}=\psi(q)b_{f}
.

Proof.

The intersection pairing is Galois-equivariant, hence

b_{f}^{\sigma}=\langle(\Xi^{k,k,j})^{\sigma},Z_{f}^{\sigma}\rangle.

The Beilinson-Flach class is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, so it is left invariant by
\sigma
. To compute
Z_{f}^{\sigma}
, we write
\sigma=\sigma_{q}
for some
q\in(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
, where
\sigma_{q}
is the unique automorphism of
\mathrm{Gal}({\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}/{\mathbb{Q}})
characterised by
\zeta_{N}\mapsto\zeta_{N}^{q}
.

In the composition defining
Z_{f}
, the only correspondence which is not defined over
\mathbb{Q}
is that induced by the Atkin-Lehner involution. According to
[
Oht95
, 75]
, the action of the Galois group on the Atkin-Lehner involution is given by composition with a diamond operator:
w_{N}^{\sigma_{q}}=w_{N}\circ\langle q\rangle
. Since
\langle q\rangle^{*}
acts on
\omega_{f}
and
\eta_{f}
as the multiplication by
\psi(q)=(\psi)_{Gal}^{-1}(\sigma_{q})
, the claim is proved.
∎

The proposition shows in particular that
b_{f}\in\bigl(H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),1+k-j)\otimes K_{f}\bigr)^{\psi}
. The comparison of
b_{f}
with the class
\varphi_{\psi}
from Theorem
5.1.5
is the main aim of the next section and will lead to our main result. To allow that, we need to show that
b_{f}
and
\varphi_{\psi}
are in the same
1
-dimensional subspace.

As stated in Theorem
5.1.5
due to Beilinson (see also the account in
[
Sou86
, §4.1]
), when
j<k

\dim(H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),1+k-j)\otimes K_{f})^{\psi}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\psi(-1)=(-1)^{k-j}$}\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}

Under our running hypothesis that
j
is even, we are always in the first case, as
\psi
and
k
necessarily have the same parity because
f
is not zero. Therefore, in this case this result suffices to ensure that
b_{f},\varphi_{\psi}
lie in the same
1
-dimensional
K_{f}
-vector space.

However, when
j=k
the above result does not apply. Furthermore, in that case the first cohomology group of
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
is infinite-dimensional. More specifically, we have

H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),1)\simeq\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})^{\times}

which is an abelian group of infinite rank. In order to exploit a
1
-dimensional eigenspace we need then to give extra conditions that imply bounds on the overall dimension. We will show in this section that we need the full integrality of the motivic classes
b_{f}
in order to restrict the dimension of the
\psi
-eigenspace. We first prove results regarding the integrality of the motivic classes, and then prove a dimension formula for the eigenspace of interest.

8.1
Integrality of
K
-elements

In this subsection we discuss integrality of Beilinson-Flach
K
-elements. The question we are addressing is whether or not they live in
(H_{\mot}^{1}(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})},1+k-j)\otimes K_{f})^{\psi}
. We separate the cases
j=k
and
j<k
.

Case
j<k

In this case
2k-2j+1\geq 3
, which entails the equality

K_{2k-2j+1}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}=K_{2k-2j+1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}.

Therefore we can regard
b_{f}
as being integral, as it already lied in the torsion-free part of the
K
-group.

Case
j=k

There are no straightforward integrality results regarding
K_{1}
that we can apply in this case, but we have an explicit equality
K_{1}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}))=\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})^{\times}
which is our starting point. Notice that the special case
k=j=0
has been originally addressed by Dasgupta. In particular, for every possible value of
k
there is at least one value of
j
such that the Beilinson-Flach
K
-element associated to those
k,j
is integral.

Notice that as
j=k
the
K
-elements live in
H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),1)\otimes K_{f}=\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})^{\times}\otimes K_{f}
, with equality given by the Kummer map. By applying the étale regulator, we have a map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})^{\times}\otimes\mathbb{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})^{\times}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\et}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\et}}

where on the right we have again used the identification given by the Kummer map. We will show that the étale realisation of
b_{f}
is in
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),S}^{\times}\otimes K_{f})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
where
\mathcal{S}
is a suitably chosen finite set of primes of
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
. This will prove that the motivic classes belong to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathcal{S}}^{\times}\otimes K_{f}
, which is the cohomology of
\Spec\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathcal{S}}
.

Recall that inside the first étale cohomology group one constructs Selmer groups. We are interested in particular in the Bloch-Kato Selmer group. Indeed, the Kummer map gives a further identification:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}^{\times}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})^{\times}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}}

which continues to hold if we relax the Bloch-Kato local condition for primes in a finite set
\mathcal{S}
. More precisely, we have the identification

H^{1}_{f,\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathcal{S}}^{\times}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}

where we use the following notation:

•

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathcal{S}}^{\times}
are the
\mathcal{S}
-units of
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
, that is the set of elements in
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
that are integral units at all primes outside
\mathcal{S}
;

•

H^{1}_{f,\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))
is the relaxed Selmer group, consisting of cohomology classes
x\in H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))
such that
\mathrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{q}}(x)\in H^{1}_{f}
for every
\mathfrak{q}\not\in\mathcal{S}
, and
\mathrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{q}}(x)\in H^{1}_{g}
for every
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{S}
.

Recall that for primes
\mathfrak{q}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.11111pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.11111pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.97221pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.97221pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}p
, locally at
\mathfrak{q}
by definition
H^{1}_{f}=H^{1}_{ur}
and
H^{1}_{g}=H^{1}
.

The statement that
b_{f}
belongs to
(\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathcal{S}}\otimes K_{f})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
is then equivalent to saying that
r_{\et}(b_{f})
belongs to
H^{1}_{f,\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))\otimes K_{f}
, as the two subgroups correspond to one another under
r_{\et}
. We will prove the following theorem.

Theorem 8.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of primes of
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
dividing
N
, then

r_{\et}(b_{f})\in H^{1}_{f,\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))\otimes K_{f}.

Equivalently,
b_{f}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathcal{S}}^{\times}\otimes K_{f}
. Moreover,
\Xi^{k,k,k}_{\et}\in H^{1}_{f,\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))
.

Proof of the theorem.

We have to show that the localisation of
r_{\et}(b_{f})
at a prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}
of
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
belongs to
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))
if
\mathfrak{q}
is outside
\mathcal{S}
, and to
H^{1}_{g}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))
if
\mathfrak{q}
is contained in
\mathcal{S}
.

First of all, by definition
r_{\et}(b_{f})
can be written as the pairing of
\Xi_{\et}^{f,f,k}
and
Z_{f}^{\et}
, where the latter belongs to
H^{0}_{\et}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)(k+1))
. In particular,
Z_{f}^{\et}
corresponds to a morphism
z\colon\mathbb{Z}\to M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)(k+1)
of
G_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}
-representations. By tensoring,
z
induces a morphism
M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k)\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1)
. Moreover, morphisms of
G_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}
-representations are also morphisms of
G_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}}}
-representations; and they commute with restriction to subgroups and scalar extensions. Therefore they respect the local unramified and Bloch-Kato conditions, meaning that:

\displaystyle x\in H^{1}_{ur}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))

\displaystyle\implies z(x)\in H^{1}_{ur}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1)),

\displaystyle x\in H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))

\displaystyle\implies z(x)\in H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1)).

Therefore, it suffices to prove that

\Xi_{\et}^{f,f,k}\in H^{1}_{f,\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))\subseteq H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k)).

We first treat the case of primes in
\mathcal{S}
: we have to show that the localisation to
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}}
of
\Xi_{\et}^{f,f,k}
is in
H^{1}_{g}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))
when
\mathfrak{q}
lies over a prime divisor of
N
. Since
\mathfrak{q}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.11111pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.11111pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.97221pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.97221pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}p
(because
p\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.11111pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.11111pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.97221pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.97221pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}N
) this is the whole cohomology group, so the thesis is trivial.

We now address the case of primes outside
\mathcal{S}
. In order to do so we build on
[
Sch90
, Theorem 1.2.4]
, which shows that
M_{\etbar}(f)
is unramified at all rational primes not dividing
pN
, and crystalline at
p
(in Scholl’s language, our
M_{\etbar}(f)
is the
p
-adic realisation of the motive denoted
M(f)
). It follows that
M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)
,
M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}
and their twists are also unramified at all rational primes away from
pN
and crystalline at
p
, since the category of
B_{\mathrm{crys}}
-admissible representations is closed by tensor products and duals.

Now
\Xi_{\et}^{f,f,k}\in H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))
, so it is automatically unramified at all primes
\mathfrak{q}\subseteq\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
not lying over a prime divisor of
pN
. On the other hand, even if the representation is crystalline, the localisation of
\Xi_{\et}^{f,f,k}
at primes over
p
is not necessarily in the local Bloch-Kato subgroup.

For the benefit of the reader we also give a second, slightly different argument to conclude that
\Xi_{\et}^{f,f,k}
is unramified at primes not dividing
pN
. From
[
Sch90
, Remark 4.2.1]
we know that
\mathscr{W}_{k}
has a smooth and proper model over
\mathbb{Z}[1/N]
. Taking into account the base change, the classes
\Xi_{\et}^{f,f,k}
are actually in
H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{S}_{p}},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))
. Here we had to add to the set
\mathcal{S}
the set
\mathcal{S}_{p}
of primes over
p
, as
M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k)
is only unramified outside
pN
. Let
\Sigma=\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{S}_{p}
and use the isomorphism

H^{1}_{\et}(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\Sigma},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))\simeq\;H^{1}(\mathrm{Gal}({\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})^{\Sigma}}/{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))

where
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})^{\Sigma}
is the maximal extension of
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
unramified outside
\Sigma
. Since in this extension all the inertia subgroups for primes outside
\Sigma
are trivial, the localisation at those primes necessarily lands into
H^{1}_{ur}
. This shows that
\Xi_{\et}^{f,f,k}
is unramified at all primes
\mathfrak{q}
not lying over a prime divisor of
pN
.

To conclude the proof, if
\mathfrak{q}\mid p
then the localisation of
\Xi_{\et}^{f,f,k}
is in the image of the Bloch-Kato exponential, by the reasoning we did at the beginning of Subsection
7.2
. Since the local Bloch-Kato subgroup coincides with the image of
\exp
, this proves the claim.
∎

The theorem shows that Beilinson-Flach
K
-elements are integral units at all primes of
\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})
that do not divide
N
.

We remark that the non-compactified and compactified Beilinson-Flach cohomology classes show the same behaviour. Indeed, if
S
is the set of rational primes dividing
N
, then the
\mathbb{Q}
-version of
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,k]}_{\et}
is in
H^{1}_{f,S}(\Spec\mathbb{Q},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))
because:

•

if
l\mid N
, then
H^{1}_{g}(\mathbb{Q}_{l},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))
equals the full
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{l},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))
so the claim is trivial;

•

if
l\not\in S
,
l\neq p
then the Bloch-Kato local condition coincides with the unramified local condition, and the non-compactified classes are unramified at
l
by the remark after Definition 3.3.2 in
[
KLZ15
]
, as
l
does not divide
pN
;

•

if
l\not\in S
,
l=p
then the localisation of
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,k]}_{\et}
is in the local Bloch-Kato subgroup as it is in the image of the Bloch-Kato exponential, by the same argument used in the proof, see also the introduction of
[
KLZ17
]
.

The precise behaviour of
r_{\et}(b_{f})
at primes lying over prime divisors of
N
is harder to investigate. Recall that since the involved representation is
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1)
, at primes not lying over
p
the unramified condition becomes:

H^{1}_{ur}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))=H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))=0.

Since the dimension of
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))
is
1
, the local unramified condition is not trivial. This is a special feature of the representation
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1)
, as for every
n\neq 1
we have
H^{1}_{ur}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(n))=H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(n))
(they are both
1
-dimensional if
n=0
, and both zero otherwise). Therefore, if
\mathfrak{q}\mid N
we want to characterise when the localisation of étale classes is zero at
\mathfrak{q}
.

Proposition 8.1.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}\mid N
, then the localisation at
\mathfrak{q}
of

\Bigl(1-\frac{p^{k}}{\alpha_{f}^{2}}\Bigr)\Bigl(1-\frac{\alpha_{f}\beta_{f}}{p^{k+1}}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigl(1-\frac{\beta_{f}^{2}}{p^{k+1}}\Bigr)\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,k]}_{\et}

belongs to
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))
.

Proof.

By the proof of
[
LZ16
, Theorem 8.1.4(i)]
, the specialisation of the three-parameter Beilinson-Flach classes
\mathcal{BF}^{[F,F]}
at
(k,k,k)
is mapped under
\mathrm{Pr}^{\alpha_{f}}\times\mathrm{Pr}^{\alpha_{f}}
to a class whose localisation is in
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))
. We underline that even though that theorem assumes that the two forms have different weights, the first point does not use this fact. We now compute this class.

According to
[
LZ16
, Theorem 5.4.2]
, the specialisation of
\mathcal{BF}^{[F,F]}
equals

\Bigl(1-\frac{p^{k}}{a_{p}(F_{k})a_{p}(F_{k})}\Bigr)\frac{\mathrm{BF}^{[F_{k},F_{k},k]}_{\et}}{(-1)^{k}k!}.

The specialisation at
k
of the Coleman family
F
equals the
p
-stabilisation of
f_{k}
, hence
a_{p}(F_{k})=\alpha_{f}
without losing generality. This explains the first Euler factor. The factor
(-1)^{k}k!
at the denominator is a non-zero scalar, so it does not change the local behaviour, and we can omit it. Notice that the above cohomology class is related to
F_{k}
, i.e. to the
p
-stabilisation of
f_{k}
rather than to
f_{k}
itself. By
[
LZ16
, Proposition 3.5.5]
, the application of
\mathrm{Pr}^{\alpha_{f}}\times\mathrm{Pr}^{\alpha_{f}}
maps

(\mathrm{Pr}^{\alpha_{f}}\times\mathrm{Pr}^{\alpha_{f}})_{*}(\mathrm{BF}^{[F_{k},F_{k},k]}_{\et})=\Bigl(1-\frac{\alpha_{f}\beta_{f}}{p^{k+1}}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigl(1-\frac{\beta_{f}^{2}}{p^{k+1}}\Bigr)\mathrm{BF}^{[f_{k},f_{k},k]}_{\et}.

This provides the missing Euler factors. This finishes the proof, since the localisation of the resulting class at
\mathfrak{q}
is in the local Bloch-Kato subgroup by the quoted theorem.
∎

Remark
.

As explained in the proof, the map
\mathrm{Pr}^{\alpha_{f}}\times\mathrm{Pr}^{\alpha_{f}}
has the effect of translating Beilinson-Flach classes related to the
p
-stabilisation of
f
, to classes related to
f
.

The proposition applies equally to the compactified étale classes
\Xi^{f,f,k}_{\et}
, as they coincide with
\mathrm{BF}^{[f,f,k]}_{\et}
, by Proposition
6.3.1
. We then obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 8.1.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}\mid N
, then the localisation at
\mathfrak{q}
of

(26)

\Bigl(1-\frac{p^{k}}{\alpha_{f}^{2}}\Bigr)\Bigl(1-\frac{\alpha_{f}\beta_{f}}{p^{k+1}}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigl(1-\frac{\beta_{f}^{2}}{p^{k+1}}\Bigr)\Xi^{f,f,k}_{\et}

belongs to
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}},M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))
.

Recall that
\Xi^{f,f,k}_{\et}
always lies in
H^{1}_{f,\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),M_{\etbar}(f\otimes f)^{*}(-k))
. From the above proposition we directly deduce

Corollary 8.1.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}\mid N
. If
\psi(p)\neq 1
and
\beta_{f}^{2}\neq p^{k+1}
, then
r_{\et}(b_{f})=0
at
\mathfrak{q}
. In particular,
r_{\et}(b_{f})\in H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))\otimes K_{f}
, equivalently
b_{f}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}^{\times}\otimes K_{f}
.

Proof.

By the same argument used in the proof of Theorem
8.1.1
, the image of a class in the (global) Selmer group under a morphism, is still in the (global) Selmer group. Since in this case the class (
26
) is in the Selmer group at
\mathfrak{q}
, the class

\Bigl(1-\frac{p^{k}}{\alpha_{f}^{2}}\Bigr)\Bigl(1-\frac{\alpha_{f}\beta_{f}}{p^{k+1}}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigl(1-\frac{\beta_{f}^{2}}{p^{k+1}}\Bigr)r_{\et}(b_{f})

is in the Selmer group at
\mathfrak{q}
too, because
r_{\et}(b_{f})
is the image of
\Xi^{f,f,k}_{\et}
under a morphism. Therefore its localisation at
\mathfrak{q}
is zero.

Under the hypotheses in the statement, all the factors multiplying
r_{\et}(b_{f})
are non-zero, so the localisation of the class itself must vanish. Indeed, the only Euler factors that could vanish are the second and the third. The second one is non-zero as
\alpha_{f}\beta_{f}=p^{k+1}\psi(p)\neq p^{k+1}
by hypothesis, and the third one directly from the hypotheses. This proves the claim.
∎

All the extra factors in equation (
26
) appear in
E(f,f,k+1)
. Hence the hypothesis
E(f,f,k+1)\neq 0
implies that all the extra factors in the statement are non-zero, so by the corollary
r_{\et}(b_{f})
vanishes in
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))
, and is in
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))
. We will use this fact in the next section.

Corollary 8.1.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}\mid N
. If
\psi(p)\neq 1
then
r_{\et}(b_{f})=0
at
\mathfrak{q}
for all but finitely many values of
k
. In particular,
b_{f}\in H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))\otimes K_{f}
for all but finitely many values of
k
, equivalently
b_{f}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}^{\times}\otimes K_{f}
for the same values of
k
.

Proof.

We use the same argument of the previous corollary. Under these hypotheses the localisation at
\mathfrak{q}
can be non-zero only if
\beta_{f}^{2}=p^{k+1}
. In this case we deduce
v_{p}(\alpha_{f})=v_{p}(\beta_{f})=(k+1)/2\leq v_{p}(a_{p}(f))
. The equality can then hold only for finitely many values of
k
, because the slope is bounded in the Coleman family as
a_{p}(F)
is a rigid function, and
a_{p}(F_{k})=\alpha_{f}
.
∎

The second Euler factor in equation (
26
) can also vanish, in particular it does so if and only if
\psi(p)=1
. If we do not impose the condition
\psi(p)\neq 1
, then we are not able to tell if the localisation of
r_{\et}(b_{f})
at
\mathfrak{q}
is zero. Contrarily to the condition
\beta_{f}^{2}=p^{k+1}
, which can hold only for finitely many cases, the condition
\psi(p)=1
can hold infinitely often, as
\psi
is constant along the family. Therefore, if we assume that
\psi(p)\neq 1
then along the
1
-dimensional slice
\{(k,k,k)\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{3}
the classes
r_{\et}(b_{f})
are in the Bloch-Kato Selmer group almost always. If we do not assume it, we can only deduce that they are in
H^{1}_{f,\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))
.

We underline the following equivalence:

(\psi(p)\neq 1)\wedge(\beta_{f}^{2}\neq p^{k+1})\iff E(f,f,k+1)\neq 0.

The last condition also appears in the hypotheses of Theorem
7.2.11
.

8.2
A dimension formula for units

As explained earlier in this section, we now prove a formula computing the dimension of the
\psi
-eigenspace in the case
j=k
. As we proved in the last subsection, Beilinson-Flach
K
-elements are
\mathcal{S}
-integral in general, and integral if we assume additional hypotheses. Combined with the character action, this shows that they are in

(H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathcal{S}},1)\otimes K_{f})^{\psi}.

In this subsection we compute the dimension of this
K_{f}
-vector space.

We will use the following isomorphism induced by the Kummer map, as explained at the beginning of Subsection
8.1
:

H^{1}_{\mot}(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathcal{S}},1)\otimes K_{f}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathcal{S}}^{\times}\otimes K_{f}.

The key result of this subsection is the following theorem computing the dimension of a character eigenspace inside the group of
\mathcal{S}
-units. It goes under the name of “equivariant Dirichlet’s units theorem”.

Theorem 8.2.1
.

Let
S\subset\mathbb{N}
be a finite set of rational primes,
\mathcal{S}
the set of primes of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N})}
lying over those in
S
,
\chi
a character modulo
N
induced by a primitive character
\check{\chi}
modulo
\check{N}
,
M
a number field containing the values of
\chi
. Then the
\chi
-eigenspace of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathcal{S}}^{\times}
has dimension:

\displaystyle\dim(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mu_{N}),\mathcal{S}}^{\times}\otimes M)^{\chi}=|\{l\in S\mid l\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.11111pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.11111pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.97221pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.97221pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mid}}}\check{N},\;\check{\chi}(l)=1\}|+d_{0}

where

\displaystyle d_{0}=\begin{cases}0&\text{$\chi$ odd or trivial}\\
1&\text{$\chi$ even and non-trivial}\end{cases}.

Theorem
8.2.1
is not an original result, but as far as the authors are aware, the only proofs present in literature are of analytical nature, and rely on the computation of the order of vanishing of the
L
-functions attached to the unit groups and their eigenspaces. The first proof is due to Tate
[
Tat84
, §3]
, who computed the multiplicity of every character inside the character of a Galois representation
V
, by recasting it as the order of vanishing of an Artin
L
-function. By applying his results to the case where
V
is the group of
\mathcal{S}
-units, the theorem follows. For a purely algebraic—and more elementary—proof for the cyclotomic case, see the first author’s PhD thesis
[
Arl20
, Theorem 8.2.1]
.

The theorem should be put in the context of the circle of ideas surrounding the Dirichlet-Chevalley-Hasse
\mathcal{S}
-units theorem, which states that if
M
